Universality in Relativistic Spinning Particle Models
Joon-Hwi Kim, Sangmin Lee

Abstract
We establish an equivalence between massive spinning particle models in four spacetime dimensions coupled to electromagnetism or gravity, within the spin-magnitude-preserving sector. Four representative models in the literature are shown to describe exactly the same physics in their free and interacting theories: vector oscillator, spinor oscillator, spherical top, and massive twistor. The Bargmann-Michel-Telegdi (BMT) and quadrupolar Mathisson-Papapetrou-Dixon (QMPD) equations are derived in a model-independent fashion. This universal framework allows for incorporating higher spin multipole interactions as well. We establish the rigorous construction of the interacting theory of the spherical top model with emphasis on spin gauge invariance. Applications to black hole physics, conserved charges, and post-Newtonian or post-Minkowskian frameworks are discussed.

KIAS-P26006

Introduction

The construction of
Lagrangian or Hamiltonian models of relativistic spinning particles
is an old yet foundational subject
in physics.
Early approaches by Frenkel
Frenkel:1926zz
and Thomas

Thomas:1927yu

date back a whole century.
A vast variety of models have appeared in the literature since then,
employing
bosonic

Mathisson:1937zz
;
Papapetrou:1951pa
;
Dixon:1970zza
;
Hanson:1974qy
;
Bailey:1975fe
;
souriau1970structure
;
souriau:1970b
;
Souriau:1974ahp
;
Kunzle:1972uk
;
Grassberger:1977tn
;
Rempel:2015foa
;
Deriglazov:2015bqa
;
Basile:2023vyg
;
Haddad:2024ebn
,
fermionic

Berezin:1976eg
;
Casalbuoni:1975hx
;
Casalbuoni:1976tz
;
Brink:1976sz
;
Brink:1976uf
,
or
spinorial/twistorial

woodhouse1997geometric
;
Lyakhovich:1996we
;
Rempel:2016jbn
;
Mezincescu:2015apa
;
Penrose:1974di
;
Perjes:1974ra
;
tod1977some
;
Bette:1989zt
;
Bette:2004ip
;
Fedoruk:2007dd
;
Deguchi:2015iuw
;
Kim:2021rda
;
ambikerr1

variables.
See also Refs.
Frydryszak:1996mu
;
Rivas:2002
;
Deriglazov:2017jub

for an overview.
A systematic geometric perspective on the subject
was pioneered by Souriau

souriau1970structure
;
souriau:1970b
;
Souriau:1974ahp
,
the significance of which is difficult to overstate;
see Ref.
Damour:2024mzo

for a recent account.

The abundance of spinning particle models
is both a blessing and a complication.
On the one hand,
model-specific features or intuitions
may be advantageous,
rendering
certain properties of
the free or interacting theories of spinning particles
more transparent.
On the other hand, they
might hinder appreciating the
underlying physical structures that are common to all implementations.
Typical models involve redundant variables encoding unphysical (i.e., gauge) degrees of freedom,
in which case model-specific calculations and derivations tend to be inefficient and less intuitive.
Translating between different models
is generically
a nontrivial task
and may involve
computing
Dirac brackets.

The latter standpoint might have been implied in the recent article
Damour:2024mzo
by Damour and Iglesias-Zemmour,
where Souriau
souriau1970structure
;
souriau:1970b
;
Souriau:1974ahp
’s
(pre)symplectic formulation of
an elementary
spinning particle
is identified as
the most economical framework
employing a minimal number of variables
whereas other formulations
are viewed as rather redundant.

Recently, Refs.
sst-asym
;
Kim:2024grz

have made useful observations
regarding
the universal, model-independent structures
of massive spinning particles
in four spacetime dimensions.
Firstly,
Ref.
sst-asym

provided a constructive proof that
a set of physical variables
(x^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})

exists in
any phase space of a free massive spinning particle
as a direct implication of global Poincaré symmetry,
realizing a universal Poisson bracket relation.
1
1

1

In Ref.
sst-asym
,
interactions are added from the perspective of scattering amplitudes at asymptotia.

Secondly,
Ref.
Kim:2024grz

noticed that
the bulk dynamics
of a massive spinning particle model
coupled to electromagnetism
can effectively be characterized by
knowing only
the Poisson brackets
between the physical variables
(x^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})

while forgetting the extra details
of the model.
The lesson is that
the interacting theories of different spinning particle models
can describe the same physics
via such consistent truncations.

By taking
these observations due to Refs.
sst-asym
;
Kim:2024grz

as input,
this paper thoroughly
establishes an equivalence between massive spinning particle models in four spacetime dimensions
with or without electromagnetic and gravitational interactions.
This identifies
a universality in spinning particle models
as a direct consequence of physical principles
such as Poincaré symmetry,
gauge invariance,
and equivalence principle.
This universality implies that
the
Bargmann-Michel-Telegdi (BMT)
Bargmann:1959gz
equations,
the
Mathisson-Papapetrou-Dixon (MPD)
Mathisson:1937zz
;
Papapetrou:1951pa
;
Dixon:1970zza

equations,
and their arbitrary higher multipole extensions
admit unified derivations within any massive spinning particle model.
We provide a consistent presentation of these ideas
in the Hamiltonian formulation
while speaking in the
languages of symplectic/Poisson geometries.

In Sec.
2
,
we reproduce Ref.
sst-asym
’s construction
to explicate the universality at the level of free theory,
reviewing the universal Poisson bracket relation between the physical variables

x^{\mu}
(position),

\hat{y}^{\mu}
(spin length),

p_{\mu}
(momentum).
We provide an additional geometric analysis
that clarifies the connection to
Souriau
souriau1970structure
’s
construction
in the context of
the coadjoint orbit

kirillov1975elements
;
kostant1970orbits
;
bacry1967space
;
arens1971classical
;
Carinena:1989uw
method.
These establish
the existence of
ten- or eight-dimensional
universal phase spaces
in any massive spinning particle model
as symplectic submanifolds.

Figure 1:

The four models are
classified by their spin phase spaces:

T^{*}S^{2}
or
T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)

up to double cover.
In this figure,
the sizes of
the white/black cores
visualize
the dimensions of
the reduced phase spaces
that involve no
unphysical spinning degrees of freedom.
The pink areas
visualize
the surplus dimensions
(number of unphysical variables)
employed by
the defining phase spaces.

Next, Sec.
3

with four concrete example systems
gathered from the literature:
vector oscillator

Grassberger:1977tn
;
Rempel:2015foa
;

woodhouse1997geometric
;

;

Hanson:1974qy
;

,
and
massive twistor

Penrose:1974di
;
Perjes:1974ra
;

;

ambikerr1

Despite the full generality of
our claims on the universality,
these four instances
represent
a notable subset
in the vast zoo of models
that is worth spotlighting
in a modern context.
We presume isotropy,
meaning that
an internal
\mathrm{SO}(3)
(“little group”) symmetry action
is present in the particle’s rest frame.

Fig.
1

provides an overview of these four models.
Hamiltonian systems exhibiting an
\mathrm{SO}(3)
symmetry
are classified
in terms of the rotational (spin) phase spaces they employ.
The sensible options are

T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)
,

T^{*}S^{2}
,
and

S^{2}

in the order of decreasing dimension,
with the understanding that
their double cover counterparts are also
considered
for spinorial implementations.
The models in the first column
employ
the four-dimensional spin phase space

T^{*}S^{2}
.
The models in the second column
employ
the six-dimensional spin phase space

T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)
.
The
spinor oscillator and massive twistor models
are the spinorial reformulations of
vector oscillator and spherical top models,
respectively.

In order to maintain relativistic covariance,
these models typically employ more variables than are strictly necessary.
Fig.
1
represents
the number of unphysical spin variables
by
the size of the pink margins,
demonstrating
the visual grammar of this paper:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\pdfliteral\hbox to0.0pt{Pink\hss}\pdfliteral\mbox{Pink}}}\,\,=\,\,\text{Unphysical,{\hskip 1.00006pt} Gauge,{\hskip 1.00006pt} Spurious,{\hskip 1.00006pt} Fake,{\hskip 1.00006pt} Redundant}\,.

One sees that
the vectorial implementations are more redundant than spinorial ones.
For example, the spherical top
involves six unphysical variables
reflecting the notorious issue of
spin supplementary conditions (SSCs)

pryce1948mass
;
Newton:1949cq
;
moller1949dynamique
;
moller1949definition
;
fleming1965covariant
;
Dixon:1970zz
;
Costa:2014nta
;
Mikoczi:2016fiy
;
Witzany:2018ahb
,
whose precise mathematical formulation
in the standard frameworks of gauge redundancies
has been achieved recently

Steinhoff:2015ksa
;
Kim:2021rda
.

Advocates of the Souriau
souriau1970structure
;
souriau:1970b
;
Souriau:1974ahp

formulation
highlight that
the smallest possible spin phase space is
the two-dimensional
S^{2}
.
The universal geometrical analysis
of Sec.
2

implies that
all of the four models in Fig.
1

achieve such
S^{2}

via appropriate reductions.
This point is explicitly substantiated in
Sec.
4

by establishing the hierarchy

T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3){\>\supset\>}T^{*}S^{2}{\>\supset\>}S^{2}

of spin phase spaces
in the Dirac bracket framework.

Figure 2:

The structure of this paper.

In Secs.

In Secs.

Having established universality in the free theory,
we then consider
interacting theories:
electromagnetism and gravity.
See Fig.
2
for an overview of our plan.

Sec.
5
establishes
universality in electromagnetic backgrounds.
It is shown that the BMT equations

Bargmann:1959gz

are directly implied by
the universal Poisson bracket relation between
the physical variables
(x,\hat{y},p)

and thus
arise in any spinning particle model.

Sec.
6
then
makes an important conceptual leap
that proposes
the existence of
the variables
(x,\hat{y},p)

and their universal Poisson bracket relation
in
general relativity
.
Based on this crucial generalization,
it is shown that the MPD equations

Mathisson:1937zz
;
Papapetrou:1951pa
;
Dixon:1970zza

and its quadrupolar extension,
which we refer to as the
quadrupolar MPD (QMPD) equations

Steinhoff:2009tk
;
Harte:2011ku
;
Vines:2016unv
;
Compere:2023alp
;
Ramond:2026fpi
;
khriplovich1989particle
;
Yee:1993ya
;
Khriplovich:1997ni
;
Thorne:1984mz
,
are direct implications of the
general-relativistic universal variables
(x,\hat{y},p)

and their Poisson algebra.

Two theoretical frameworks play critical roles
in these discussions.
The first is

Souriau’s method of minimal coupling

souriau1970structure
;
souriau:1970b
;
Souriau:1974ahp
,
where
interactions are implemented by
modifications of the symplectic structure
instead of the Hamiltonian.
This approach has been also known as
the “Feynman bracket” method in the literature

dyson1990feynman
;
lee1990feynman
;
tanimura1992relativistic
;
stern1993deformed
;
chou1994dynamical
;
berard1999dirac

and has been systematized and generalized under the name “symplectic perturbation theory”:
see Ref.
csg
and also Ref.
ambikerr1
.
Brief reviews of this method are provided
at the start of Secs.
5
and
6
.

The second is

the covariant Poisson bracket

csg
,
which characterizes Poisson structures
in a manifestly covariant fashion
that never writes down bare Christoffel symbols or connection coefficients
at every intermediate step.
To our best knowledge,
this idea is due to Ref.
csg

while its precursors may trace back to
Refs.
souriau1970structure
;
dewitt1965dynamical
;
DeWitt:2011nnj
;
vanHolten:1992bu
;
Gibbons:1993ap
;
dAmbrosi:2015ndl
.
This paper does not assume prior familiarity with Ref.
csg
,
where a comprehensive formalization is provided
in terms of
almost-symplectic geometry
and Ehresmann connections.

To explicitly prove the existence of the general-relativistic universal variables postulated by Sec.
6
,
we perform a thorough investigation on
the covariant phase space geometries of
the four models
in Secs.
7
and
8
.
The spherical top model exhibits an exceptional degree of complexity
due to its
spin gauge redundancies
;
hence it is discussed separately in Sec.
7
.

Since Dixon
Dixon:1970zza
,
it has been well-known that
the definition of center coordinates
for extended relativistic bodies
is intrinsically tied to the problem of
separating the total angular momentum
into orbital and spin parts.
This point gives rise to
the aforementioned issue of SSCs,
addressed during the derivations of the MPD equations

Mathisson:1937zz
;
Papapetrou:1951pa
;
Dixon:1970zza
;
tulczyjew1959motion
;
dixon1964covariant

and investigated throughout
Refs.
pryce1948mass
;
Newton:1949cq
;
moller1949dynamique
;
moller1949definition
;
fleming1965covariant
;
Dixon:1970zz
;
see
Refs.
Costa:2014nta
;
Mikoczi:2016fiy
;
Witzany:2018ahb

for recent reviews.
Steinhoff
Steinhoff:2015ksa

has established
the modern reformulation of SSCs
as gauge redundancies
in the spherical top model
at the level of free theory,
while Ref.
Kim:2021rda
provided
a subtle yet crucial refinement.

To our knowledge, however,
the status of this reformulation has not been established at the level of interacting theory.
Importantly, the interaction action must be spin gauge invariant
for physical observables to be independent of one’s customary choice of a SSC.
Sec.
7
of this paper,
for the first time,
establishes such spin gauge invariant couplings
and explicitly identifies the spin gauge-invariant center coordinates in general relativity
by exploiting the all-order geodesic deviation formalism of Ref.
gde

that builds upon the Synge framework
Ruse:1931ht
;
Synge:1931zz
;
Poisson:2011nh
;
Vines:2014oba
.
This rigorous formulation of the interacting spherical top
reveals that the addition of “fictitious terms”
in the Lagrangian
is mandatory in noncovariant SSCs.

The subject of relativistic spinning particle models
has gained revived interest
in the recent gravitational physics community.
The context is the point-particle effective theory of astrophysical objects

Goldberger:2004jt
;
Porto:2005ac
;
Levi:2015msa
;
Porto:2016pyg
;
Levi:2018nxp
,
the applications of which
range from post-Newtonian (PN) or post-Minkowskian (PM) approximations

Will:2011nz
;
Blanchet:2013haa
;
Levi:2018nxp
;
Damour:2016gwp
;
Cheung:2018wkq
;
Cristofoli:2019neg
;
Bjerrum-Bohr:2021din
;
Kalin:2020mvi
;
Kalin:2020fhe
;
Mogull:2020sak
;
Jakobsen:2021smu
;
Bjerrum-Bohr:2022blt
;
Vines:2017hyw

to
extreme mass-ratio inspirals
Cheung:2023lnj
;
Cui:2025bgu
;
Skoupy:2025nie
;
Chen:2025ncm
.
The most intriguing yet challenging problem
may have been
the construction of worldline models
that can exactly represent spinning black holes
to all orders.
The all-orders effective actions of

\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
,
Kerr, and Kerr-Newman probes
are established by
Refs.
gmoov
;

;
njmagic.1
;
njmagic.11
.

With this context in mind,
we end by showcasing the applications of our constructions
in Sec.
9
.
Sec.
9.1

approaches dynamical symmetries and conserved charges
directly from the universal variables,
in light of a recent surge of interest on the topic

Compere:2021kjz
;

;

while commenting on

Sec.

Sec.

explains
how the universal variables
(x,\hat{y},p)
and their Poisson algebra
admit a remarkable complex-geometrical description
via
the spinspacetime program of Ref.
sst-asym
,
realizing
the Newman-Janis
Newman:1965tw-janis

property
in terms of complex coordinates “
z{\>=\>}x{\>+\,}i\hat{y}
”

sst-asym
;
gmoov
;
probe-nj
;
njmagic.1
;
njmagic.11
.
Keywords include exact
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
equations of motion (EoM)
and
dynamical Newman-Janis shift of conserved charges.

Overall, this work should provide a solid foundation for relativistic spinning particle models and
offer clear guidance for future research in point-particle effective theory.

2
Universality in Free Theory

This section reviews a set of crucial facts shown by Ref.
sst-asym
,
which enables direct identifications of
the universal geometrical structures
of massive spinning-particle phase spaces.
An additional analysis on symplectic leaves is provided.

2.1
Universal Poisson Bracket Relation from Poincaré Symmetry

Suppose any Hamiltonian formulation of
a massive physical system
that enjoys global Poincaré symmetry.
Ref.
sst-asym
shows that
one can always construct
functions

\hat{x}^{\mu}
,
\hat{y}^{\mu}
,
p_{\mu}

on its phase space
\mathds{P}

such that

p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hat{x}=0=p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hat{y}

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\hat{x}^{\mu}},{\hat{x}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,\,&=\,\,\frac{1}{-p^{2}}\,\Big(\,{\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{x}^{\mu}p^{\nu}{-\,}p^{\mu}\hat{x}^{\nu}+\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\hat{y}_{\rho}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\sigma}}\,\Big)\,,\\
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\hat{x}^{\mu}},{\hat{y}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,\,&=\,\,\frac{1}{-p^{2}}\,\hat{y}^{\mu}p^{\nu}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\hat{y}^{\mu}},{\hat{y}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\frac{1}{-p^{2}}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\hat{y}_{\rho}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\sigma}\,,\\
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\hat{x}^{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,\,&=\,\,\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}-\frac{p^{\mu}\hskip 0.50003ptp_{\nu}}{p^{2}}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\hat{y}^{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0\,.\end{split}

(1)

Here,
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}
denotes the Poisson bracket
between functions
f
and
g
on the phase space
\mathds{P}
.
This result applies to not only discrete systems but also continuous systems:
a compact mass distribution on flat spacetime
with a conserved stress-energy
T^{\mu\nu}
, for instance.
Yet, the interest of this paper will be restricted specifically to one-particle systems.

Firstly,
Poincaré symmetry
implies that
one can construct
the Poincaré charges
as functions on the phase space
\mathds{P}
:
momentum
p_{\mu}
and total angular momentum
J^{\mu\nu}
.
They must satisfy the following Poisson brackets
as per the definition of the Poincaré algebra:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{J^{\mu\nu}},{J^{\rho\sigma}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,\,&=\,\,J^{\mu\rho}\hskip 1.00006pt\eta^{\nu\sigma}-J^{\mu\sigma}\hskip 1.00006pt\eta^{\nu\rho}+J^{\nu\sigma}\hskip 1.00006pt\eta^{\mu\rho}-J^{\nu\rho}\hskip 1.00006pt\eta^{\mu\sigma}\,,\\[1.79993pt]
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{J^{\mu\nu}},{p_{\rho}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,\,&=\,\,\delta^{\mu}{}_{\rho}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\nu}-\delta^{\nu}{}_{\rho}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\mu}\,,\\[1.79993pt]
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,\,&=\,\,0\,.\end{split}

(2)

Here,
\eta^{\mu\nu}
is the flat inverse metric
of signature
(-,+,+,+)
.
The system being massive means that its classical states are not allowed to approach the zero locus of
p^{2}

in
\mathds{P}
.

Secondly,

define

\hat{x}^{\mu}
and
\hat{y}^{\nu}
as

\displaystyle\hat{x}^{\mu}\,:=\,\frac{1}{p^{2}}\,J^{\mu\nu}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\nu}\,,\quad\hat{y}^{\mu}\,:=\,\frac{1}{-p^{2}}\,{*}J^{\mu\nu}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\nu}\,,

(3)

where
{*}J^{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}J_{\rho\sigma}

describes the Hodge star
in the convention
\varepsilon_{0123}=+1
.
By direct computation,
one shows that
the Poincaré algebra in Eq. (
2
)
implies
the Poisson brackets in Eq. (
1
)
between
\hat{x}^{\mu}
,
\hat{y}^{\mu}
, and
p_{\mu}
.
Also,

p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hat{x}=0=p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hat{y}

by the antisymmetry of
J^{\mu\nu}
and
{*}J^{\mu\nu}
.
This concludes the proof.

On account of the well-known definition of the Pauli-Lubanski pseudovector,
the variable
\hat{y}^{\mu}

in Eq. (
3
)
describes the spin pseudovector
normalized in units of length.
In this paper,
we refer to this
\hat{y}^{\mu}

as
spin length pseudovector
.

In fact,

\hat{y}^{\mu}
can evaluate to zero in some systems;
take a free relativistic scalar particle
formulated in the cotangent bundle of Minkowski space,
for instance.
In this paper,
we concern systems with nontrivial
\hat{y}^{\mu}
only.

2.2
Partly Reduced Formulation

Physically,
the notion of mass dimension must exist in the phase space
\mathds{P}
.
Especially, the momentum
p_{\mu}

carries mass dimension
+1

while the angular momentum
J^{\mu\nu}

carries mass dimension
0
.
This implies that the dilatation charge
D

would also exist
as a function on the phase space
\mathds{P}
,
such that the Poisson bracket with
D

measures the mass dimension:

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{\mu}},{D}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,+p_{\mu}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{J^{\mu\nu}},{D}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0\,.

(4)

Ref.
sst-asym
shows that
Eqs. (
2
) and (
4
)
together implies the existence of
functions
x^{\mu}
,
\hat{y}^{\mu}
, and
p_{\mu}

on
\mathds{P}

such that

p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hat{y}=0

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{x^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,\,&=\,\,\frac{1}{-p^{2}}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\hat{y}_{\rho}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\sigma}\,=\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\hat{y}^{\mu}},{\hat{y}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,,\\
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{\hat{y}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,\,&=\,\,\frac{1}{-p^{2}}\,\Big(\,{\hat{y}^{\mu}p^{\nu}+p^{\mu}\hat{y}^{\nu}}\,\Big)\,,\\[1.79993pt]
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,\,&=\,\,\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\hat{y}^{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0\,.\end{split}

(5)

The proof is straightforward.
Define

\displaystyle x^{\mu}\,:=\,\frac{1}{p^{2}}\,\Big(\,{J^{\mu\nu}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\nu}-D\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\mu}}\,\Big)\,,

(6)

which adds an additional term to
\hat{x}^{\mu}
.
Then direct computation verifies Eq. (
5
).

In terms of
x^{\mu}
,
\hat{y}^{\mu}
, and
p_{\mu}
,
the total angular momentum
J^{\mu\nu}

and the dilatation charge
D

are expressed as

\displaystyle J^{\mu\nu}\,=\,x^{\mu}p^{\nu}{-\,}p^{\mu}x^{\nu}+\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\hat{y}_{\rho}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\sigma}\,,\quad D\,=\,-p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}x\,.

(7)

From Eq. (
7
)
and the Poisson bracket
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}=\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}
in
Eq. (
5
),
it is evident that
the interpretation of
x^{\mu}

is a reconstructed notion of
flat spacetime coordinates.

Again, the above result applies to any massive system.
In the context of
effective point-particle description of
compact bodies,

x^{\mu}
in Eq. (
6
)
describes the Poincaré-covariant center coordinates.
Accordingly,
Eq. (
7
)
describes the covariant split
of the total angular momentum
J^{\mu\nu}

to orbital
(
x^{\mu}p^{\nu}{-\,}p^{\mu}x^{\nu}
)
and
spin
(
\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\hat{y}_{\rho}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\sigma}
)
parts.

2.3
Symplectic Leaves

Lastly, let us comprehend the above results
from a mathematical perspective.

A Poisson manifold is a manifold equipped with a Poisson bracket

\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}=\Pi(df,dg)

between functions
f
and
g
,
which arises from a bivector
\Pi
.
A symplectic manifold is a manifold equipped with a symplectic form
\omega
,
which is a closed nondegenerate two-form.
Symplectic manifolds are Poisson manifolds, but the converse is not true.
This is because the Poisson bivector
\Pi
can be degenerate,
unlike the symplectic form
\omega
.

Yet,
any Poisson manifold is foliated by

symplectic leaves
,
which are
(connected)
symplectic submanifolds of maximal dimension.
The dimension of symplectic leaves
is determined by the rank of the Poisson bivector
\Pi
.

Provided that
\hat{y}^{\mu}
is nontrivial,

\hat{x}^{\mu}
,
\hat{y}^{\mu}
, and
p_{\mu}

in Eq. (
1
)
together describes
a
10
-dimensional Poisson submanifold embedded in the phase space
\mathds{P}
.
Its symplectic leaves are
8
-dimensional.
To see this,
regard
the Poisson brackets in Eq. (
1
)
as components of an antisymmetric matrix
and compute its rank:
8
.
Furthermore, examining its kernel shows that
each of such symplectic leaves
is the locus of

\displaystyle-p^{2}\,=\,m^{2}\,,\quad-p^{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}^{2}\,=\,w^{2}\,,

(8)

where
m
and
w
are constants.
Physically,
the first equation computes the
rest mass

m

whereas
the second equation computes the
spin magnitude

w
.

In this way, it follows that
one can always identify an
8
-dimensional symplectic submanifold
\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{8}(m,w)

inside any phase space
\mathds{P}

of a massive system
with nontrivial
\hat{y}^{\mu}
.
This symplectic submanifold
will be referred to as the
8-dimensional universal phase space

of the massive spinning system,
on which the rest mass and the spin magnitude
are fixed to constant values
m
and
w
:

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\,\,\supset\,\,\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{8}(m,w)\,.

(9)

In turn,
8
sets the minimal bound
for the dimension of a massive spinning phase space.
It achieves three translational degrees of freedom and one spinning degree of freedom.
It can be seen that

\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{8}(m,w)
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{6}{\,\times\,}S^{2}
,
which
describes the coadjoint orbit

kirillov1975elements
;
kostant1970orbits
;
souriau1970structure

of the Poincaré group

souriau1970structure
;
bacry1967space
;
arens1971classical
;
Carinena:1989uw
.
It follows that

\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{8}(m,w)
is equivalent to
Souriau’s “
8
-dimensional space of motion”
reviewed in Ref.
Damour:2024mzo
.

Similarly,

x^{\mu}
,
\hat{y}^{\mu}
, and
p_{\mu}

in Eq. (
5
)
together describes a
11
-dimensional
Poisson submanifold in
\mathds{P}

whose symplectic leaves are
10
-dimensional.
Computation shows that
each symplectic leaf is the locus of

-p^{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}^{2}=w^{2}
.
This identifies the
10-dimensional universal phase space

\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{10}(w)
,
on which the spin magnitude
is fixed to a constant value
w
:

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\,\,\supset\,\,\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{10}(w)\,.

(10)

It can be seen that
\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{10}(m,w)

is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{8}{\,\times\,}S^{2}
.

It is possible to reproduce
the
8
-dimensional universal phase space in Eq. (
9
)
from the
10
-dimensional universal phase space in Eq. (
10
):

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{8}(m,w)\,\,=\,\,\Big\{\hskip 1.00006pt{(x,\hat{y},p)\in\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{10}(w)\,\Big|\,\hskip 0.50003ptp^{2}+m^{2}=0}\,\Big\}\hskip 0.50003pt\Big/\hskip-1.00006pt\hskip-0.50003pt\sim\,.

(11)

This describes a quotient along
one-parameter orbits
in
\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{10}(w)
,

\displaystyle\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{x^{\mu},\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}^{\mu},\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\mu}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,\,\sim\,\,\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{x^{\mu}+k\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\mu},\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}^{\mu},\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\mu}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,,

(12)

which is the Hamiltonian flow of
p^{2}+m^{2}
.
Physically, Eq. (
12
) implements a translation of the reconstructed spacetime coordinates in Eq. (
6
)
along the momentum direction.

Mathematically,
quotients of the form Eq. (
11
)
could be called

symplectic quotient
:
impose a constraint
and
quotient by its Hamiltonian flow

marsden1974reduction
;
meyer1973symmetries
.
The process of reducing a symplectic manifold to a smaller one
via symplectic quotient
will be referred to as
symplectic reduction
.
Clearly,
symplectic reduction always reduces the dimension
by an even number.

The physicists’ toolkit for symplectic reduction
is known as

Dirac bracket

Dirac:1950pj
;
dirac1964lectures
;
Henneaux:1992ig
.
In this paper,
we use Dirac brackets to
explicitly realize symplectic quotients
as symplectic submanifolds.
See App.
A
for a review of symplectic reduction and Dirac bracket.

It is a simple exercise to show that
the universal phase space

\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{8}(m,w)
in Eq. (
9
)
is a symplectic submanifold of

\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{10}(w)
in Eq. (
10
),
by constructing the Dirac bracket
that fixes
p^{2}+m^{2}
and
p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}x
to zero
within
\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{10}(w)
:

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\,\,\supset\,\,\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{10}(w)\,\,\supset\,\,\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{8}(m,w)\,.

(13)

Surely, this reduction recipe
applies universally in every massive spinning system.

3
The Four Models

In Sec.
2
,
we have employed results due to Ref.
sst-asym

to establish
the universal existence of
physical variables
x^{\mu}
,
\hat{x}^{\mu}
,
\hat{y}^{\mu}
, and
p_{\mu}
,
satisfying universal Poisson bracket relations
dictated by Poincaré symmetry.
In turn, the symplectic submanifolds

\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{10}(w)
and
\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{8}(m,w)

arise
universally,
characterized by
mass
m
or spin magnitude
w
.

The objective of this section is to demonstrate
this universal construction
for the four concrete example systems in Fig.
1
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}1.\,\,\,\text{
Vector Oscillator
(VO)
}&\,,\quad 3.\,\,\,\text{
Spherical Top
(ST)
},\\
2.\,\,\,\text{
Spinor Oscillator
(SO)
}&\,,\quad 4.\,\,\,\text{
Massive Twistor
(MT)
}.\end{split}

Firstly, the formulae in Eqs. (
3
) and (
6
)
are applied to
validate the universal Poisson bracket relations.
Secondly,
the universal Poisson bracket relations are reproduced as Dirac brackets
via concrete identifications of constraints.
We restrict our attention to free theory,
and wait to add on interactions in later sections.

The four models above
are defined on symplectic manifolds
with varying dimensions:

\displaystyle\mathds{P}_{16}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}\,,\quad\mathds{P}_{12}^{\mathrm{SO}}\,,\quad\mathds{P}_{20}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}\,,\quad\mathds{P}_{16}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}\,.

(14)

These models tend to involve unphysical spinning degrees of freedom
for the sake of manifest relativistic covariance,
except in the case of spinor oscillator.
After appropriate reduction procedures,
the phase spaces in Eq. (
14
) are boiled down to

\displaystyle\mathds{P}_{12}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}\,,\quad\mathds{P}_{12}^{\mathrm{SO}}\,,\quad\mathds{P}_{14}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}\,,\quad\mathds{P}_{14}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}\,,

(15)

respectively.
It then follows that

\displaystyle\mathds{P}_{12}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}\,\,\cong\,\,\mathds{P}_{12}^{\mathrm{SO}}\big/\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbb{Z}_{2}\,,\quad\mathds{P}_{14}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}\,\,\cong\,\,\mathds{P}_{14}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}\big/\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbb{Z}_{2}\,,

(16)

establishing equivalences up to double cover.

It is worth mentioning that

none
of the physical phase spaces in Eq. (
15
)
necessitate fixed spin magnitude.
On a related note,
we will assume strictly constant mass
m

when imposing the mass-shell constraint,
although
one could incorporate the Regge trajectory
Hanson:1974qy

in principle;
see App.
B
for a further exploration on this point.

For the reader’s sake,
we recapitulate the key formulae in Sec.
2
.
It suffices to identify

\displaystyle x^{\mu}\,:=\,\frac{1}{p^{2}}\,\Big(\,{J^{\mu\nu}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\nu}-D\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\mu}}\,\Big)\,,\quad\hat{y}^{\mu}\,:=\,-\frac{1}{p^{2}}\,{*}J^{\mu\nu}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\nu}\,,\quad p_{\mu}\,,

(17)

as
\hat{x}^{\mu}=x^{\mu}-p^{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptp{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}x/p^{2}

is automatic by projection of
x^{\mu}
along
p^{\mu}
.

3.1
Vector Oscillator

Our first example is
the vector oscillator model,
which has been discovered multiple times over the years

Grassberger:1977tn
;
Rempel:2015foa
;
Deriglazov:2015bqa
;
Basile:2023vyg
;
Haddad:2024ebn
.
The idea of this model is
to implement the spin angular momentum
as a composite variable
arising from a pair of vectors,
just like orbital angular momentum.

Definition

Let
\mathbb{M}=(\mathbb{R}^{4},\eta)

denote the Minkowski space
equipped with
(-,+,+,+)
-signature flat metric
\eta_{\mu\nu}
.
The phase space of the vector oscillator model is

\displaystyle\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}_{16}\,\,=\,\,\hskip-1.00006pt\Big(\,{T^{*}\oplus T\oplus T^{*}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbb{M}\,,

(18)

which has dimension
4+4+4+4=16
.
This is a direct sum bundle
with real coordinates

\displaystyle\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu},\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\mu},\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{u}}^{\mu},\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{v}}_{\mu}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,.

(19)

Here,
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}
coordinatize the base manifold
\mathbb{M}

while
p_{\mu}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{u}}^{\mu}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{v}}_{\mu}
coordinatize the fibers.
In this precise geometric sense,
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}
are external degrees of freedom
whereas
p_{\mu}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{u}}^{\mu}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{v}}_{\mu}
are internal degrees of freedom.
The symplectic potential is given by

\displaystyle\theta\,=\,p_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}+{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{v}}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{u}}^{\mu}\,,

(20)

so the nonvanishing components of the Poisson bracket are

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{u}}^{\mu}},{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{v}}_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\,.

(21)

A symplectic potential is a one-form whose exterior derivative is the symplectic form
\omega=d\theta
.
The pointwise inverse
of the symplectic form
is the Poisson bivector.
The components of the Poisson bivector are the Poisson brackets.
In this way, Eq. (
20
) derives Eq. (
21
).

The spin sector is more conveniently described
in terms of complex variables

{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}=({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{u}}^{\mu}+i{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{v}}^{\mu})/\hskip-1.00006pt\sqrt{2}
,
in which case
the phase space is recast as

\displaystyle\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}_{16}\,\,\cong\,\,\hskip-1.00006pt\Big(\,{T^{*}\oplus T^{\mathbb{C}}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbb{M}\,.

(22)

Here,
T^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{M}
denotes the complexified tangent bundle of
\mathbb{M}
.
The coordinates are now

\displaystyle\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu},\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\mu},\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,,

(23)

while the symplectic potential reads

\displaystyle\theta\,=\,p_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}+\frac{i}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptd\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}-d\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}}\,\Big)\,.

(24)

Now the nonvanishing components of the Poisson bracket are identified as

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}},{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,-i\hskip 1.00006pt\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\,,

(25)

so
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}_{\mu}
could serve as
lowering and raising operators in the quantum theory.

Universality

Firstly, we apply Eq. (
17
)
to construct the physical variables

(x^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})
.

The Poincaré and dilatation charges
are readily derived by
implementing Noether theorem
in the Hamiltonian framework.
Due to the geometrical identification in Eq. (
22
),
spacetime translations act on the phase space
\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}_{16}
as

{\delta}_{\xi}({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu},p_{\mu},{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu})=({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt}\xi^{\mu},p_{\mu},{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu})
.
The momentum is the generator of this transformation,
which is simply
p_{\mu}
.
Lorentz transformations, on the other hand,
rotate all variables as

{\delta}_{\varepsilon}({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu},p_{\mu},{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu})=(\varepsilon^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu},-p_{\nu}\hskip 1.00006pt\varepsilon^{\nu}{}_{\mu},

\varepsilon^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\nu})
,
where

\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}=-\varepsilon^{\nu\mu}

is infinitesimal.
From the symplectic potential in Eq. (
24
),
it follows that this transformation is generated by

-\frac{1}{2}\,\varepsilon_{\mu\nu}\hskip 1.00006ptJ^{\mu\nu}
,
where

\displaystyle J^{\mu\nu}\,=\,\Big(\,{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}p^{\nu}-p^{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\nu}}\,\Big)+i\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}^{\nu}-{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\nu}}\,\Big)\,.

(26)

Meanwhile, the mass dimensions of
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}
,
p_{\mu}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}
are
-1
,
1
, and
0
, respectively.
Thus, the dilatation charge is

D=-p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}
.

From the above identification of the Poincaré and dilatation charges,
Eq. (
17
) implies

\displaystyle x^{\mu}\,=\,{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}+\frac{i}{p^{2}}\,\Big(\,{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}^{\nu}-{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\nu}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\nu}\,,\quad\hat{y}^{\mu}\,=\,\frac{i}{-p^{2}}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{\nu}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}_{\rho}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}_{\sigma}\,.

(27)

Direct computation using Eqs. (
25
) and (
27
) verifies that
Eqs. (
1
) and (
5
) are reproduced in the vector oscillator model,
substantiating the claims in Sec.
2
.

Eq. (
27
) shows that
the basic spacetime variable
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}

provided by the model
is mismatched from the physical center coordinates
x^{\mu}
.
Indeed,
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}},{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}=0
while
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{x^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\neq 0
.
Below, we refer to
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}
as spurious center coordinates.

Dirac Brackets

Secondly,
we perform the Dirac bracket analysis.
The question here is whether imposing appropriate constraints on the phase space
\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}_{16}

reproduces Dirac brackets that agree with
the universal Poisson brackets in Eq. (
5
).

Six constraints have been identified
in the literature

Rempel:2015foa
;
Rempel:2016jbn
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi_{1}\,=\,p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}\,,&\quad\Psi_{2}\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\,{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{2}\,,\quad\Psi_{3}\,=\,{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}-w\,,\\
\bar{\Psi}_{1}\,=\,p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}\,,&\quad\bar{\Psi}_{2}\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\,{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}^{2}\,,\quad\Psi_{4}\,=\,\frac{1}{2i}\log\hskip-0.50003pt\left(\frac{\eta{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}}{\eta{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}}\right)\,.\end{split}

(28)

Here,
\eta^{\mu}
is a constant spacelike reference vector.
It is known that

(\Psi_{2},\bar{\Psi}_{2},\Psi_{3})
form an
\mathrm{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
algebra under the Poisson bracket
Haddad:2024ebn
.
The constraints
(\Psi_{1},\bar{\Psi}_{1})

shift the spurious center
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}

by their Hamiltonian actions,
while the other constraints cannot.

The geometry of the internal phase space starts from
the unconstrained
T^{*}\mathbb{M}\cong\mathbb{M}^{\mathbb{C}}

and is reduced as

T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{3}\supset T^{*}S^{2}\supset S^{2}

via successive impositions of

(\Psi_{1},\bar{\Psi}_{1})
,

(\Psi_{2},\bar{\Psi}_{2})
,
and
(\Psi_{3},\Psi_{4})
.
The imposition of
(\Psi_{1},\bar{\Psi}_{1})
is a strict necessity,
as it sets
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}=x^{\mu}

such that the ambiguities in defining the center position is removed.
Meanwhile, from physical grounds, the imposition of
(\Psi_{2},\bar{\Psi}_{2})

would be also obligatory
since it kills
translational modes
in
T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{3}

which apparently bear no clear physical significance.
The internal phase spaces
T^{*}S^{2}
and
S^{2}
, on the other hand,
nicely qualify as rotational phase spaces.

Consequently, the following series of reductions arises
for the vector oscillator model:

\displaystyle\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}_{16}\,\supset\,\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}_{12}\,\supset\,\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}_{10}(w)\,\supset\,\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}_{8}(m,w)\,.

(29)

Here,
\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}_{12}
imposes
(\Psi_{1},\bar{\Psi}_{1},\Psi_{2},\bar{\Psi}_{2})

while
\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}_{10}(w)
imposes all constraints in Eq. (
28
).
The last reduction is via the constraint pair

(p^{2}{\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt}m^{2},p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}x)

as discussed around Eq. (
13
).

The derivation of the Dirac brackets
is not difficult.
For instance,
on
\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}_{12}
one finds

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}},{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{12}\,=\,\frac{1}{-p^{2}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}^{\mu\nu}&\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{12}\,=\,\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\,,\\
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}},{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{12}\,=\,\frac{1}{-p^{2}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\nu}&\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}},{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{12}\,=\,-i\,\bigg(\,{\hat{\delta}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}-\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}_{\nu}}{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}}}\,\bigg)\,,\end{split}

(30)

where

{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}^{\mu\nu}:=i\hskip 1.00006pt({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}^{\nu}-{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\nu})
.

It follows that
the Dirac brackets between

({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})

take the same form as
the Poisson brackets between
(x^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})

in Eq. (
5
)
on
\mathds{P}_{12}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}
or
\mathds{P}_{10}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}(w)
,
for instance.
Similarly,
the Dirac brackets between

({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})

take the same form as
the Poisson brackets between
(\hat{x}^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})

in Eq. (
5
)
on the fully reduced
\mathds{P}_{8}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}(m,w)
.
This establishes that
the universal phase spaces
in Sec.
2

are concretely realized in the vector oscillator model as

\displaystyle\mathds{P}_{10}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}(w)\,\cong\,\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{10}(w)\,,\quad\mathds{P}_{8}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}(m,w)\,\cong\,\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{8}(m,w)\,.

(31)

3.2
Spinor Oscillator

Our second example is
the spinor oscillator model.
This model is explicitly formulated in
Woodhouse
woodhouse1997geometric

(see pp. 117-119),
for instance,
and appears also in Refs.
Lyakhovich:1996we
;
Rempel:2016jbn
.
The idea is to realize the spin phase space
S^{2}

as the Riemann sphere
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
,
in terms of an internal two-spinor variable
\zeta_{\alpha}
.
This is essentially the
relativistic generalization of the construction

jordan1935zusammenhang
;
Schwinger1952NYO3071

widely known under the name of
“Schwinger’s oscillator model of spin.”

Definition

The phase space of
the spinor oscillator model
is

\displaystyle\mathds{P}^{\mathrm{SO}}_{12}\,\,=\,\,\hskip-1.00006pt\Big(\,{T^{*}\oplus\mathscr{S}^{-}_{\smash{\hskip-0.40974pt\hskip-0.81949pt\times}}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbb{M}\,,

(32)

where
\mathscr{S}^{-}_{\smash{\hskip-0.40974pt\hskip-0.81949pt\times}}\mathbb{M}

is the left-handed spinor bundle over
the flat spacetime
\mathbb{M}

with zero section removed.
That is, the typical fiber of
\mathscr{S}^{-}_{\smash{\hskip-0.40974pt\hskip-0.81949pt\times}}\mathbb{M}

is
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\times}:=\mathbb{C}^{2}\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt\{0\}
.
The phase space in Eq. (
32
) has
4+4+4=12
real dimensions
and is coordinatized by

\displaystyle\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{x^{\mu},\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\mu},\hskip 1.00006pt\zeta_{\alpha}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,,

(33)

where
\zeta_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\times}
.
The symplectic potential is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\theta\,=\,p_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2i}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\zeta}_{\dot{{\alpha}}}\,\hat{p}^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}d\zeta_{\alpha}-d\bar{\zeta}_{\dot{{\alpha}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{p}^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}\hskip 0.50003pt\zeta_{\alpha}}\,\Big)\,,\end{split}

(34)

where
\hat{p}_{\mu}{\,:=\,}p_{\mu}/|p|

and
|p|{\,:=\,}(-p^{2})^{1/2}
.
The nonzero components of the Poisson bracket are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{x^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\frac{1}{-p^{2}}\,S^{\mu\nu}&\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{\zeta_{\alpha}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\frac{(\sigma^{\mu\nu}\zeta)_{\alpha}}{2p^{2}}\,p_{\nu}\,,\\
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}&\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\zeta_{\alpha}},{\bar{\zeta}_{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,i\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{p}_{{\alpha}{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\,.\end{split}

(35)

Our spinor conventions are identical to Ref.
Kim:2021rda
.

S^{\mu\nu}
is a composite variable
defined as

\displaystyle S^{\mu\nu}\,:=\,\frac{1}{4i}\,\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\zeta}_{\dot{{\alpha}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\hskip 1.00006pt(\bar{\sigma}^{\mu\nu})^{\dot{{\alpha}}}{}_{\dot{{\beta}}}\,\hat{p}^{{\dot{{\beta}}}{\alpha}}\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{p}^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\beta}}\hskip 1.00006pt(\sigma^{\mu\nu})_{\beta}{}^{\alpha}\hskip 1.00006pt}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006pt\zeta_{\alpha}\,.

(36)

As a Reformulation

The spinor oscillator model
is essentially a reformulation
of the vector oscillator model in Sec.
3.1

(cf. Ref.
Rempel:2016jbn
).
Recall the four constraints

(\Psi_{1},\bar{\Psi}_{1},\Psi_{2},\bar{\Psi}_{2})

in Eq. (
28
)
that achieved the
12
-dimensional
\mathds{P}_{12}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}
in Eq. (
29
).
The constraints
\Psi_{2}{\>=\>}0
and
\bar{\Psi}_{2}{\>=\>}0

are explicitly solved by
a spinorial parametrization of the form

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}\,=\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2w}}\,\xi^{\dot{{\alpha}}}\zeta^{\alpha}\,.

(37)

To impose

\Psi_{1}{\>=\>}0
and
\bar{\Psi}_{1}{\>=\>}0

as well,
one stipulates the relation
2
2

2

This declares a Euclidean conjugation relationship
between
\xi^{\dot{{\alpha}}}
and
\zeta_{\alpha}

in the rest frame of
\hat{p}^{\mu}
.
The sign choice in Eq. (
38
) sets
-\hat{p}^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}\doteq\delta^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}
in the rest frame
in our conventions.
Also,
\zeta^{\alpha}=+\xi_{\dot{{\beta}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{p}^{{\dot{{\beta}}}{\alpha}}
.

\displaystyle\xi^{\dot{{\alpha}}}\,=\,-\hat{p}^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}\hskip 1.00006pt\zeta_{\alpha}\,.

(38)

At this stage,
we are free to take any one of the spinor variables

\zeta_{\alpha}
and
\xi^{\dot{{\alpha}}}

as fundamental
and regard the other as dependent.

By plugging in Eq. (
37
)
to
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}
given in Eq. (
24
),
renaming
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}
to
x^{\mu}
,
and discarding a total derivative,
one finds

\displaystyle\begin{split}\theta\,=\,p_{\mu}\hskip 0.50003ptdx^{\mu}+\frac{i}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\bar{\xi}^{\alpha}\hskip 1.00006ptd\zeta_{\alpha}-\hskip 0.50003ptd\bar{\zeta}_{\dot{{\alpha}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\xi^{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\,\Big)\,.\end{split}

(39)

This readily reproduces Eq. (
34
)
upon eliminating
\xi^{\dot{{\alpha}}}
via Eq. (
38
).
Mathematically, this implements an explicit embedding
\mathds{P}_{12}^{\mathrm{SO}}\to\mathds{P}_{16}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}

and its pullback.

In this sense,
the phase space
\mathds{P}^{\mathrm{SO}}_{12}
of the spinor oscillator model
in Eq. (
32
)
provides a spinorial reformulation of the
constrained phase space
\mathds{P}_{12}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}

of the vector oscillator model
in Eq. (
29
)
by solving

(\Psi_{1},\bar{\Psi}_{1},\Psi_{2},\bar{\Psi}_{2})

explicitly.
Their precise relation is given in Eq. (
16
),
which arises from
the double cover relationship between
the spin phase spaces

\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\times}
and
T^{*}S^{2}{\hskip 1.00006pt\setminus\hskip 1.00006pt}S^{2}
;
consider the representation of a two-spinor as a flag

penrose:1985spinors1
,
for instance.

This equivalence up to double cover
is established again by the fact that
the Poisson brackets
between
(x^{\mu},p_{\mu},{\alpha}^{\mu},\bar{\alpha}_{\mu})

in the spinor oscillator model
reproduces the Dirac brackets
of the vector oscillator model
in Eq. (
30
)
via Eqs. (
37
) and (
38
).

As a side note,
we may want to remark on
a chiral approach
that can be useful in some calculations.
The idea is to take
the left-handed (anti-self-dual) spinors

\zeta_{\alpha}
and
\bar{\xi}^{\alpha}

as fundamental variables,
which yet sacrifices
manifest reality of the symplectic structure.
Then the symplectic potential in Eq. (
39
)
becomes

\displaystyle\theta\,=\,p_{{\alpha}{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\hskip 0.50003ptd\bigg(\,{x^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{i}{4|p|}\,\Big(\,{\xi^{\dot{{\alpha}}}\bar{\xi}^{\alpha}-\bar{\zeta}^{\dot{{\alpha}}}\zeta^{\alpha}}\,\Big)\hskip-1.00006pt}\,\bigg)+i\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\xi}^{\alpha}\hskip 1.00006ptd\zeta_{\alpha}\,.

(40a)

If instead the right-handed (self-dual) spinors

\xi^{\dot{{\alpha}}}
and
\bar{\zeta}_{\dot{{\alpha}}}

are taken as fundamental,
the symplectic potential in Eq. (
39
)
becomes

\displaystyle\theta\,=\,p_{{\alpha}{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\hskip 0.50003ptd\bigg(\,{x^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{i}{4|p|}\,\Big(\,{\xi^{\dot{{\alpha}}}\bar{\xi}^{\alpha}-\bar{\zeta}^{\dot{{\alpha}}}\zeta^{\alpha}}\,\Big)\hskip-1.00006pt}\,\bigg)-i\hskip 1.00006ptd\bar{\zeta}_{\dot{{\alpha}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\xi^{\dot{{\alpha}}}\,.

(40b)

By using any of Eqs. (
40a
) or (
40b
),
the symplectic form can be
presented in the Darboux format
at the expense of manifest reality.

Universality

Now
we apply Eq. (
17
)
to identify
the physical variables

(x^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})
.

Working similarly as in Sec.
3.1
,
the Poincaré and dilatation charges
are found as

\displaystyle p_{\mu}\,,\quad J^{\mu\nu}\,=\,\Big(\,{x^{\mu}p^{\nu}-x^{\nu}p^{\mu}}\,\Big)+S^{\mu\nu}\,,\quad D\,=\,-p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}x\,,

(41)

where
S^{\mu\nu}
is given in Eq. (
36
).
From Eqs. (
17
) and (
41
),
it follows that
the default position variable
x^{\mu}

of the spinor oscillator model
is exactly the physical center coordinates,
as the notation has already suggested.
The spin length pseudovector is

\displaystyle\hat{y}^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}\,=\,-\frac{1}{4|p|}\,\Big(\,{\xi^{\dot{{\alpha}}}\bar{\xi}^{\alpha}-\bar{\zeta}^{\dot{{\alpha}}}\zeta^{\alpha}}\,\Big)\,,

(42)

so Eq. (
36
) describes

S^{\mu\nu}=\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}_{\rho}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\sigma}
.

Direct computation using Eqs. (
35
) and (
42
)
verifies that
Eqs. (
1
) and (
5
)
are reproduced in the spinor oscillator model,
substantiating the claims in Sec.
2
.

Dirac Brackets

Next,
we perform the Dirac bracket analysis.

The constraint pair relevant for fixing the spin magnitude can be identified as

\displaystyle\phi_{1}\,=\,\bar{\xi}^{\alpha}\zeta_{\alpha}-2w\,,\quad\chi^{1}\,=\,\frac{i}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\log\bigg(\,{\frac{\langle\eta\zeta\rangle}{\langle\bar{\xi}\eta\rangle}}\,\bigg)\,,

(43)

with the aid of a constant reference spinor
\eta^{\alpha}
.
Here,
\phi_{1}
generates
\mathrm{U}(1)
orbits
in the space of
\zeta_{\alpha}
.
Consequently, the following series of reductions arises for the spinor oscillator model:

\displaystyle\mathds{P}_{12}^{\mathrm{SO}}\,\supset\,\mathds{P}_{10}^{\mathrm{SO}}(w)\,\supset\,\mathds{P}_{8}^{\mathrm{SO}}(m,w)\,.

(44)

For instance, the Dirac bracket on
\mathds{P}_{10}^{\mathrm{SO}}(m,w)
is found as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{x^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{10}\,=\,\frac{1}{-p^{2}}\,S^{\mu\nu}&\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{\zeta_{\alpha}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{10}\,=\,\frac{(\sigma^{\mu\nu}\zeta)_{\alpha}}{2p^{2}}\,p_{\nu}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\zeta_{\alpha}},{\zeta_{\beta}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{10}\,=\,\frac{i}{4}\,\epsilon_{{\alpha}{\beta}}\,,\\
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{10}\,=\,\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}&\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\zeta_{\alpha}},{\bar{\zeta}_{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{10}\,=\,i\hskip 1.00006pt\bigg(\,{\hat{p}_{{\alpha}{\dot{{\alpha}}}}-\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{\zeta_{\alpha}\hskip 1.00006pt\eta^{\beta}\hat{p}_{{\beta}{\dot{{\alpha}}}}}{{\kern-0.37003pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{0.99}[0.99]{{\hbox{{${\lfloor\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\lfloor$}}}}$}}}}}}}$}\kern-0.39993pt}\bar{\zeta}|\hat{p}|\eta\rangle}-\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{\eta_{\alpha}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\zeta}_{\dot{{\alpha}}}}{\langle\eta\zeta\rangle}}\,\bigg)\,.\end{split}

(45)

It is left as an exercise to verify that
the Poisson brackets between

(x^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})

reproduce Eq. (
5
)
in
\mathds{P}_{12}^{\mathrm{SO}}
.
Since
(x^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})
are invariant under the
\mathrm{U}(1)
rephasing of
\zeta_{\alpha}
,
this implies that
the Dirac brackets between

(x^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})

on
\mathds{P}_{10}^{\mathrm{SO}}

also reproduces Eq. (
5
).
Similarly,
the Dirac brackets between

(x^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})

reproduce Eq. (
1
)
on
\mathds{P}_{10}^{\mathrm{SO}}(m,w)
.
Therefore,
the universal phase spaces
in Sec.
2

are concretely realized in the spinor oscillator model as

\displaystyle\mathds{P}_{10}^{\mathrm{SO}}(w)\,\cong\,\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{10}(w)\,,\quad\mathds{P}_{8}^{\mathrm{SO}}(m,w)\,\cong\,\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{8}(m,w)\,.

(46)

Note the absence of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
quotients here.

3.3
Spherical Top

Our third example is
the spherical top model,
introduced by Hanson and Regge
Hanson:1974qy

in 1974.
The idea of this model is to pursue a smooth conceptual extension of
rigid body dynamics in Newtonian mechanics
by generalizing the Eulerian angles—as coordinates on the group manifold
\mathrm{SO}(3)
—to a Lorentz frame.

Concretely, let
\mathrm{SO}^{+}\hskip-0.50003pt(1,3)
denote the restricted Lorentz group,
and
let
\mathbb{M}\rtimes\mathrm{SO}^{+}\hskip-0.50003pt(1,3)
be the restricted Poincaré group.
The phase space of the spherical top model is
the cotangent bundle of the restricted Poincaré group,
which has dimension
2\times(4+6)=20
:

\displaystyle\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}_{20}\,\,=\,\,\hskip-1.00006ptT^{*}(\mathbb{M}\rtimes\mathrm{SO}^{+}\hskip-0.50003pt(1,3))\,.

(47)

The coordinates are

\displaystyle\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu},\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{A},\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\mu},\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\mu\nu}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,,

(48)

where
({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu},{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{A})\in\mathbb{M}{\,\rtimes\,}\mathrm{SO}(1,3)

coordinatize the base group manifold
while
(p_{\mu},{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\mu\nu})\in\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{M}{\,\rtimes\,}\mathrm{SO}^{+}\hskip-0.50003pt(1,3))

coordinatize the cotangent fibers.

{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{A}{\>\in\>}\mathrm{SO}^{+}\hskip-0.50003pt(1,3)
is a restricted Lorentz group element,
dubbed body frame.

{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\mu\nu}{\>=\>}{-{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\nu\mu}}
is a Lorentz algebra element.

Figure 3:

A demonstration of a Lorentz-violating SSC,
due to Fleming
fleming1965covariant
.
Suppose a perfectly symmetric rotating sphere
whose geometric center is at rest
in an inertial frame.
Suppose an observer
that moves with a constant relative velocity.
In the observer’s frame,
the apparent center of energy
(defined in terms of the energy density
T^{00}
),

{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}
,
is displaced from the geometric center.
Namely, the Lorentz factor
describes that
the fast-moving parts of the sphere
appear heavy,
while the slow-moving parts appear light.
However,
the physical center
x

has always been
the geometric center.
The mismatch between
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}
and
x

is
because the observer has
defined
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}

in a way that involves their own four-velocity vector
u^{\mu}_{\text{obs}}
,
which is a Lorentz-violating reference:

T^{00}=T_{\mu\nu}\hskip 0.50003ptu_{\text{obs}}^{\mu}u_{\text{obs}}^{\nu}
.
This demonstrates the fakeness or arbitrariness of the spurious center
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}

in noncovariant SSCs.

The symplectic potential reads

\displaystyle\theta\,=\,p_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\,{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\mu\nu}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{A}\hskip 1.00006ptd\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\nu}{}_{B}\hskip 1.00006pt\eta^{AB}\,,

(49)

so the nonvanishing components of the Poisson bracket are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,\,&=\,\,\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\,,\\
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{A}},{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\rho\sigma}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,\,&=\,\,\Big(\,{-2\hskip 1.00006pt\delta^{\mu}{}_{[\rho}\eta_{\sigma]\nu}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\nu}{}_{A}\,,\\
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\mu\nu}},{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\rho\sigma}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,\,&=\,\,\Big(\,{-4\hskip 1.00006pt\delta^{[\kappa}{}_{[\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt\eta_{\nu][\rho}\hskip 1.00006pt\delta^{\lambda]}{}_{\sigma]}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\kappa\lambda}\,.\end{split}

(50)

The spherical top model notoriously
suffers from
unphysical redundancies,
often discussed in the context of
SSCs

pryce1948mass
;
Newton:1949cq
;
moller1949dynamique
;
moller1949definition
;
fleming1965covariant
;
Dixon:1970zz
;
Steinhoff:2015ksa
;
see Fig.
3
.
Crucially,
the generalization from
\mathrm{SO}(3)
to
\mathrm{SO}^{+}\hskip-0.50003pt(1,3)

doubles the number of spinning variables
for the sake of relativistic covariance,
introducing fake
boost
degrees of freedom.
Three of the six components of

{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{A}

are unphysical;
three of the six components of

{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\mu\nu}

are also unphysical.
In particular,
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\mu\nu}

does not faithfully represent the physical spin angular momentum of the system,
the intricacy of which leads to
ambiguities in
the definition of the center position,
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}
.
Below, we refer to

{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{A}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\mu\nu}

respectively as
spurious position,
body frame,
and
spin tensor.

Universality

Firstly,
we apply Eq. (
17
)
to construct the physical variables

(x^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})
.

The Poincaré charges
are uniquely determined from
the natural Poincaré group action
(left action)
on the cotangent bundle

\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}_{20}=T^{*}(\mathbb{M}\rtimes\mathrm{SO}^{+}\hskip-0.50003pt(1,3))
.
Meanwhile, the dilatation charge simply
follows from the mass dimensions:

-1
,
0
,
+1
, and
0

for
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{A}
,
p_{\mu}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\mu\nu}
.
The results are

\displaystyle p_{\mu}\,,\quad J^{\mu\nu}\,=\,\Big(\,{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}p^{\nu}-{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\nu}p^{\mu}}\,\Big)+{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}^{\mu\nu}\,,\quad D\,=\,-p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}\,.

(51)

From the above identification of the Poincaré and dilatation charges,
Eq. (
17
) implies

\displaystyle x^{\mu}\,=\,{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}+\frac{1}{p^{2}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}^{\mu\nu}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\nu}\,,\quad\hat{y}^{\mu}\,=\,\frac{1}{-p^{2}}\,{*}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}^{\mu\nu}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\nu}\,.

(52)

Direct computation using Eqs. (
50
) and (
52
) verifies that
Eqs. (
1
) and (
5
) are reproduced in the spherical top model,
substantiating the claims in Sec.
2
.

Dirac Brackets

Secondly,
we perform the Dirac bracket analysis.

The three spurious degrees of freedom
in the spherical top model
have been addressed since
the original work
Hanson:1974qy
.
However,
their precise mathematical formulation
within the well-established framework of gauge redundancies
was only facilitated by
Steinhoff
Steinhoff:2015ksa
.
Upon a slight yet important refinement
Kim:2021rda
,
the three gauge generators are identified as

\displaystyle\phi_{a}\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\,\Big(\,{\hat{p}^{\mu}+\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{0}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\mu\nu}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\nu}{}_{a}{\quad\implies\quad}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\phi_{a}},{\phi_{b}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0\,,

(53)

where
a\in\{1,2,3\}

describes the spatial part of the internal index
A\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.
The vanishing Poisson bracket in Eq. (
53
)
establishes that the gauge algebra is abelian,
so the gauge orbits form
\mathbb{R}^{3}

Kim:2021rda
.
To remove all spurious spinning degrees of freedom,
one takes the symplectic quotient

\displaystyle\mathds{P}_{14}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}\,\,=\,\,\Big\{\hskip 1.00006pt{({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}},p,{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}})\in\mathds{P}_{20}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}\,\Big|\,\hskip 0.50003pt\phi_{a}=0}\,\Big\}\hskip 0.50003pt\Big/\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbb{R}^{3}\,.

(54)

In this framework,
SSCs
are elegantly reformulated as gauge-fixing conditions

Steinhoff:2015ksa
.
For example, consider
the gauge-fixing functions

\displaystyle\text{Covariant Gauge}\,:\quad{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\chi}}}^{a}\,=\,\hat{p}_{\mu}\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu a}\,.

(55)

Imposing both
\phi_{a}{\>=\>}0
and
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\chi}}}^{a}{\>=\>}0

not only
implements the covariant SSC

{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\mu\nu}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\nu}{\>=\>}0

but also aligns
the spurious body frame
with a rest frame of the momentum:

{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{0}{\>=\>}\hat{p}^{\mu}
.
Hence
three boost components are removed
from both
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\mu\nu}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{A}

as desired.
In this gauge fixing,
the spurious center coincides with the physical center.

For another example,
consider the gauge-fixing functions

\displaystyle\text{Canonical Gauge}\,:\quad{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\chi}}}^{a}\,=\,l_{\mu}\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu a}\,,

(56)

where
l^{\mu}
is
a constant vector that is
unit and timelike:
l^{2}{\>=\>}{-1}
.
Imposing both
\phi_{a}{\>=\>}0
and
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\chi}}}^{a}{\>=\>}0

now implements

{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\mu\nu}\hskip 0.50003pt(\hat{p}^{\nu}{\>+\>}l^{\nu})=0
,
which is known as the Pryce-Newton-Wigner

pryce1948mass
;
Newton:1949cq

SSC.
As will be revisited in Sec.
9.3
,
this is the SSC
canonically
utilized in post-Newtonian calculations:
the constant unit timelike vector
is naturally interpreted as
the four-velocity
of an inertial observer
so that

{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{0}=l^{\mu}=u^{\mu}_{\text{obs}}
.
Evidently, this observer four-velocity
is an explicit Lorentz-violating reference structure.
Thus, in this gauge choice,
Lorentz symmetry
is spuriously broken down
to the spatial
\mathrm{SO}(3)
isotropy in the observer’s rest frame.
See also the appendix of Ref.
sst-asym

for an insightful “bootstrap” derivation of this gauge-fixing condition.

Any choice
for the three gauge-fixing functions
establishes
the following series of symplectic reductions arises for the spherical top model:

\displaystyle\mathds{P}_{20}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}\,\supset\,\mathds{P}_{14}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}\,\supset\,\mathds{P}_{12}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}(m)\,.

(57)

The Dirac brackets on
\mathds{P}_{14}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}
and
\mathds{P}_{12}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}(m)

have been well-described
in various gauges

Hanson:1974qy
;
Kim:2021rda
;
Lee:2023nkx
.
If one adopts the covariant gauge condition in Eq. (
55
),
the Dirac bracket
on
\mathds{P}_{14}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}

describes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}},{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{14}\,=\,\frac{1}{-p^{2}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}^{\mu\nu}&\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}},{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\rho\sigma}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{14}\,=\,\frac{1}{p^{2}}\,2\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}^{\mu}{}_{\smash{[\rho}\vphantom{{\beta}}}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\smash{\sigma]}\vphantom{{\beta}}}\,,\\
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{14}\,=\,\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}&\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\mu\nu}},{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\rho\sigma}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{14}\,=\,\Big(\,{-4\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{\delta}^{[\kappa}{}_{[\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{\eta}_{\nu][\rho}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{\delta}^{\lambda]}{}_{\sigma]}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\kappa\lambda}\,,\\
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{a}},{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{14}\,=\,\frac{1}{p^{2}}\,p^{\mu}\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\nu}{}_{a}&\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{a}},{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\rho\sigma}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{14}\,=\,\Big(\,{-2\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{\delta}^{\mu}{}_{[\rho}\hat{\eta}_{\sigma]\nu}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\nu}{}_{a}\,,\end{split}

(58)

where
\hat{\delta}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}{\>:=\>}\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}-p^{\mu}p_{\nu}/p^{2}

and
\hat{\eta}_{\mu\nu}{\>:=\>}\eta_{\mu\nu}-p_{\mu}p_{\nu}/p^{2}
.
Computation shows that
the Dirac brackets between
({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})

due to Eq. (
58
)
take the same form as
the universal Poisson brackets in Eq. (
5
).
Similarly,
one can verify that
the Dirac brackets between
({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})

on
\mathds{P}_{12}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}

take the same form as
Eq. (
1
).

To elaborate,
the first two rows in Eq. (
58
)
directly reproduce the universal Poisson algebra.
The last row in Eq. (
58
),
on the other hand,
encodes extra information
only available for
T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)
models.
Also, note that
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{a}},{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\rho\sigma}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{14}
in Eq. (
58
)
boils down to

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{a}},{\hat{y}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{14}\,

\displaystyle=\,\frac{1}{p^{2}}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}{}_{\rho\sigma}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\rho}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\sigma}{}_{a}\,.

(59)

The spin magnitude
has not been fixed so far.
By further reductions of the spin phase space
described in Sec.
4
,
one constructs submanifolds of
\mathds{P}_{14}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}
and
\mathds{P}_{12}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}(m)
,
such as

\displaystyle\mathds{P}_{10}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}(w)\,\cong\,\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{10}(w)\,,\quad\mathds{P}_{8}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}(m,w)\,\cong\,\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{8}(m,w)

(60)

that provide explicit embeddings of the universal phase spaces in Sec.
2
.

3.4
Massive Twistor

Our fourth example is
the massive twistor model,
which traces back to
the ’70s twistor particle program
of Penrose and Perjés

Penrose:1974di
;
Perjes:1974ra
;
tod1977some
.
While this model
has been revisited several times
in Refs.
Bette:1989zt
;
Bette:2004ip
;
Fedoruk:2007dd
;
Deguchi:2015iuw
,
we follow the modern formulation put forward by Refs.
Kim:2021rda
;
ambikerr1
,
which has provided a proper interpretation of the internal symmetries.

It is now widely known that
the configurations of a massive spinning particle
can be represented by the massive spinor-helicity variable
\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}

ahh2017
.
The idea of the massive twistor model
is to
take the space of massive spinor-helicity variables
as the configuration space
so that its cotangent bundle
serves as the phase space
of a massive spinning particle.

Definition

The precise definition of
the phase space of the massive twistor model is

\displaystyle\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}_{16}\,\,=\,\,\mathbb{C}^{8}\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt\Delta\,,

(61)

where
\Delta
is a quadric in
\mathbb{C}^{8}
.
This phase space has
16
real dimensions
and is coordinatized by
8
complex variables,

\displaystyle\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I},\hskip 1.00006pt\mu^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}I}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,.

(62)

Here,
{\alpha}{\>\in\>}\{0,1\}
and
{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\>\in\>}\{0,1\}
are
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
spinor indices,
while
I{\>\in\>}\{0,1\}
is an
\mathrm{SU}(2)
spinor index.
The quadric
\Delta
removes the degenerate locus

\det(\lambda)=0
, so

\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\,\in\,\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{C})\,,\quad\mu^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}I}\,\in\,\mathbb{C}^{4}\,.

(63)

The symplectic potential is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\theta\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\,\Big(\,{\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}\hskip 1.00006ptd\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}-d\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}\hskip 1.00006pt\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}}\,\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\,\Big(\,{d\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mu^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}I}-\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\hskip 1.00006ptd\mu^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}I}}\,\Big)\,,\end{split}

(64)

which describes that
(\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I},\mu^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}I})

are canonical (Darboux) coordinates
in the complex sense.
Thus,
the nonzero components of the Poisson bracket are simply

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}},{\bar{\mu}_{J}{}^{\beta}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\delta_{I}{}^{J}\hskip 1.00006pt\delta_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}\,.\end{split}

(65)

As usual,
we omit components related by taking complex conjugations:

[\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}]^{*}{\>=\>}\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{{\alpha}}}}

and

[\mu^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}I}]^{*}{\>=\>}\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}
.
3
3

3

The
\mathrm{SU}(2)
spinor representation is pseudoreal,
meaning that an invariant tensor
\delta_{I\bar{I}}

is involved in the complex conjugation.

It should be clear from Eqs. (
64
) and (
65
)
that
\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}_{16}
can be viewed as
the cotangent bundle of the space
\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{C})
of massive spinor-helicity variables.

At first sight, the massive twistor model
seems disparate from
any of the three spinning particle models
discussed in Secs.
3.1
,
3.2
, and
3.3
.
Especially,
Eqs. (
61
) and (
65
) are purely based on spinorial variables
so that
the notion of spacetime seems completely absent.
Hence one could wonder how this model can even
achieve a conventional picture of
a particle in spacetime.
Notably, however, Ref.
sst-asym
’s construction
shown in Eq. (
17
)
reconstructs and identifies spacetime
in any phase space with Poincaré symmetry,
as is demonstrated below.

Symmetries

We start by releasing more details
on the phase space
in terms of its symmetries,
although the reader is encouraged to skip the following
and directly jump to Eq. (
67
).
The twistor space
could be
defined as the representation space
\mathbb{C}^{2,2}
of the conformal group
\mathrm{SU}(2,2)
,
which is a Kähler vector space

penrose:maccallum
;
tod1977some
.
The complex linear space
\mathbb{C}^{8}
in Eq. (
61
)
has denoted
the product
(\mathbb{C}^{2,2})^{2}
of two twistor spaces.
To see this,
consider

\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{A}}{}^{I}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\\
i\hskip 0.24994pt\mu^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}I}\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad{\bar{Z}}_{I}{}^{\mathrm{A}}\,=\,\Big(\,{-i\hskip 0.50003pt\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}\,\,\,\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{{\alpha}}}}}\,\Big)\,,

(66)

which identifies
two twistors (Dirac spinors)
Z_{\mathrm{A}}{}^{0}
and
Z_{\mathrm{A}}{}^{1}
.
In Eq. (
66
),
the
(2,2)
-signature metric
A^{\bar{{\mathrm{B}}}{\mathrm{A}}}
of the twistor space
facilitates the conjugation

{\bar{Z}}_{I}{}^{\mathrm{A}}=[Z_{\mathrm{B}}{}^{I}]^{*}\hskip 1.00006ptA^{\bar{{\mathrm{B}}}{\mathrm{A}}}
.
Note that the Poisson bracket relation in Eq. (
65
)
translates to
i\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt{Z_{\mathrm{A}}{}^{I}},{{\bar{Z}}_{J}{}^{\mathrm{B}}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}=\delta_{\mathrm{A}}{}^{\mathrm{B}}\hskip 1.00006pt\delta_{J}{}^{I}
.

On the linear space of
Z_{\mathrm{A}}{}^{I}
,
the conformal group
\mathrm{SU}(2,2)

acts from the left
(i.e., on the Dirac spinor index
{\mathrm{A}}
)
while
an
\mathrm{SU}(2)
internal symmetry
acts from the right
(i.e., on the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
spinor index
I
).
There is also
a total
\mathrm{U}(1)
rephasing transformation

Z_{\mathrm{A}}{}^{I}\mapsto e^{i\psi/2}\hskip 0.50003ptZ_{\mathrm{A}}{}^{I}
.
The interpretation established by Refs.
