Kim:2021rda
;
ambikerr1

states that
the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
is global
(massive little group)
while the
\mathrm{U}(1)
is merely gauge.
Meanwhile,
the fifteen Noether charges of
the
\mathrm{SU}(2,2)
group action
arise from
the traceless bilinear

Z_{\mathrm{A}}{}^{I}{\bar{Z}}_{I}{}^{\mathrm{B}}-\frac{1}{4}\hskip 1.00006pt\delta_{\mathrm{A}}{}^{\mathrm{B}}\hskip 1.00006pt({\bar{Z}}_{K}{}^{\mathrm{C}}Z_{\mathrm{C}}{}^{K})
.
These encode spacetime transformations
and derive Eq. (
67
).

Universality

Let us apply Eq. (
17
)
to construct
the universal variables
(x^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})
.
This analysis was performed by Ref.
sst-asym
.

The conformal group action
\mathrm{SU}(2,2)
on
\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}_{16}

includes Poincaré and dilatation
transformations.
This uniquely stipulates
the Poincaré and dilatation charges as

Kim:2021rda
;
ambikerr1

\displaystyle\begin{split}p_{{\alpha}{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\hskip 1.00006pt=\hskip 1.00006pt-\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\,,\,\,\,\,J^{\dot{{\alpha}}}{}_{\dot{{\beta}}}\hskip 1.00006pt=\hskip 1.00006pt\mu^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}I}\hskip 0.50003pt\bar{\lambda}_{\smash{I{\dot{{\beta}}}}\vphantom{{\beta}}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\delta^{\dot{{\alpha}}}{}_{\dot{{\beta}}}\,\mu^{{\dot{{\gamma}}}I}\hskip 0.50003pt\bar{\lambda}_{\smash{I{\dot{{\gamma}}}}\vphantom{{\beta}}}\,,\,\,\,\,D\hskip 1.00006pt=\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{1}{2}\,\Big(\,{\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mu^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}I}+\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}\hskip 1.00006pt\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}}\,\Big)\,.\end{split}

(67)

Meanwhile, in the spinor notation,
Eq. (
17
)
boils down to

\displaystyle x^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}+i\hskip 0.50003pt\hat{y}^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}\,=\,-\Big(\,{J^{\dot{{\alpha}}}{}_{\dot{{\beta}}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\delta^{\dot{{\alpha}}}{}_{\dot{{\beta}}}\hskip 1.00006ptD}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006pt(p^{-1})^{{\dot{{\beta}}}{\alpha}}\,.

(68)

By plugging in Eq. (
67
),
one identifies the equation

\displaystyle x^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}+i\hskip 0.50003pty^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}\,=\,\mu^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}I}\hskip 1.00006pt(\lambda^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}\,,

(69)

which describes

x^{\mu}+iy^{\mu}

in the vector notation.
It follows that the real part
x^{\mu}
is the physical spacetime coordinates,
while the imaginary part
y^{\mu}
yields the physical spin length pseudovector
\hat{y}^{\mu}

via the projection
\hat{y}^{\mu}=y^{\mu}-p^{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptp{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}y/p^{2}
.
Eqs. (
65
) and (
69
)
verify
Eqs. (
1
) and (
5
)
in the massive twistor model,
substantiating the claims in Sec.
2
.

Note that Eq. (
69
) can be written as

\displaystyle\mu^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}I}\,=\,\Big(\,{x^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}+i\hskip 0.50003pty^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006pt\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\,,

(70)

which is known as the massive generalization of
incidence relation.
The incidence relation is a central equation in twistor theory that establishes the relationship between
twistor space and (complex) spacetime

penrose1967twistoralgebra
.
Amusingly,
Ref.
sst-asym
’s
derivation of Eq. (
69
)
rediscovers the massive incidence relation,
verifying that
the complexified spacetime in twistor particle theory

penrose:maccallum
;
newman1974curiosity
;
Shirafuji:1983zd

unifies spacetime and spin as real and imaginary parts.

As a Reformulation

The massive twistor model is essentially a reformulation of the spherical top model
in Sec.
3.3
.
To see this,
recall the constraints
\phi_{a}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\chi}}}^{a}

in Eqs. (
53
) and (
55
)
that derived the constrained phase space
\mathds{P}_{14}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}{\>\subset\>}\mathds{P}_{20}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}
.
Notably, these constraints
are explicitly solved by the following parametrization:

\displaystyle\begin{split}p_{{\alpha}{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\hskip 1.00006pt=\hskip 1.00006pt-\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\,,\,\,\,\,\,\,{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}{}_{a}\hskip 1.00006pt\propto\hskip 1.00006pt(\bar{\lambda}^{-1})^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}I}\hskip 1.00006pt(\sigma_{a})_{I}{}^{J}\hskip 0.50003pt(\lambda^{-1})_{J}{}^{\alpha}\,,\,\,\,\,\,\,{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\mu\nu}\hskip 1.00006pt=\hskip 1.00006pt\varepsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\hskip 1.00006pty^{\rho}\hskip 0.50003ptp^{\sigma}\,.\end{split}

(71)

The first two spinorial parametrizations in Eq. (
71
)
trivialize
the orthogonality
p_{\mu}\hskip 0.50003pt\Lambda^{\mu}{}_{a}=0
.
In fact, they precisely describe the well-known
parametrization of
massive momentum
p_{\mu}

and polarization vectors
\Lambda^{\mu}{}_{a=1,2,3}

in terms of massive spinor-helicity variables
\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}
.
In the meantime,
the last parametrization in Eq. (
71
)
trivializes the covariant SSC
S_{\mu\nu}\hskip 0.50003ptp^{\nu}=0

in terms of the pseudovector variable
y^{\mu}
.

Yet, these are at the expense of two additional redundancies.
First,
Eq. (
71
) is invariant under
the
\mathrm{U}(1)
rephasing transformation

\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\sim e^{i\psi/2}\hskip 1.00006pt\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}
.
Second,
Eq. (
71
) is invariant under
the shift transformation

y^{\mu}\sim y^{\mu}+k\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\mu}

for
k{\>\in\>}\mathbb{R}
.

By plugging in
Eq. (
71
)
to Eq. (
49
)
and renaming
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}
to
x^{\mu}
,
one obtains

\displaystyle\theta\,=\,-\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\,dx^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}+i\hskip 1.00006pty^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\hskip 0.50003ptd\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{{\alpha}}}}{\,-\,}d\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{{\alpha}}}}}\,\Big)\,,

(72)

defined on the

16
-dimensional space of

x^{\mu}{\>\in\>}\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
y^{\mu}{\>\in\>}\mathbb{R}^{4}
, and
\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}{\>\in\>}\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{C})
:

4+4+8
.
It is easy to show that Eq. (
72
)
is equivalent to the symplectic potential in Eq. (
64
)
via the massive incidence relation in Eq. (
70
).
It should be clear that
Eq. (
64
) has computed
a pullback due to
an embedding

\mathds{P}_{14}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}\to\mathds{P}_{20}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}
.

In sum,
the massive twistor model can be regarded as
a reformulation of the spherical top
that arises by solving the constraints
for the covariant SSC,
explicitly in a spinorial fashion.
Their precise relation is given in Eq. (
16
),
which arises from
the double cover relationship between
the spin phase spaces

T^{*}\mathrm{SU}(2)
and
T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)
.
It could be helpful to note that

\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{C})\supset\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})\supset\mathrm{SU}(2)
.
In Refs.
Kim:2021rda
;
ambikerr1
,
this equivalence up to double cover
is established by
matching between the Dirac brackets
in the spherical top and massive twistor models
via Eq. (
71
).

This realization provides an alternative view on
the massive twistor model
that emphasizes the spacetime picture.
Namely,
there exists
an invertible diffeomorphism
that maps

\mathbb{C}^{8}\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt\Delta
in Eq. (
61
)
to a direct sum bundle over Minkowski space:

\displaystyle\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}_{16}\,\,\cong\,\,\Big(\,{T\oplus\mathscr{S}_{2}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbb{M}\,.

(73)

Here,

\mathscr{S}_{2}\mathbb{M}
denotes a fiber bundle over
\mathbb{M}

whose typical fiber is
\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{C})
:
massive spinor-helicity bundle over spacetime.
That is, Eq. (
73
)
is coordinatized by

\displaystyle\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{x^{\mu},\hskip 1.00006pty^{\mu},\hskip 1.00006pt\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,.

(74)

Concretely, the invertible diffeomorphism
from
(T\oplus\mathscr{S}_{2})\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbb{M}

to
\mathbb{C}^{8}\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt\Delta

is
(x^{\mu},y^{\mu},\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I})\mapsto{(\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I},(x{\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt}iy)^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}\hskip 1.00006pt\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I})}
,
which provides a mathematical formalization
for the massive incidence relation in Eq. (
70
).
4
4

4

In twistor-theoretic terms,
Eq. (
73
)
describes the massive analog of the correspondence space.
Physically, working in this massive correspondence space
is what Refs.
Shirafuji:1983zd
;
Fedoruk:2007dd

mean by the “hybrid” description.

The one-form in Eq. (
72
)
is the pullback of the one-form in Eq. (
64
)
by this diffeomorphism.

Dirac Brackets

The above analysis leads to a clear identification of the constraints of massive twistor model.
The unphysical redundancy is the
\mathrm{U}(1)
redundancy,
generated by

\displaystyle\phi_{1}\,=\,-\frac{1}{2}\,{\bar{Z}}_{I}{}^{\mathrm{A}}Z_{\mathrm{A}}{}^{I}\,=\,\frac{1}{2i}\,\Big(\,{\hskip 0.50003pt\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mu^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}I}-\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}\hskip 1.00006pt\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}}\,\Big)\,=\,-p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}y\,.

(75)

The resulting symplectic quotient is

\displaystyle\mathds{P}_{14}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}\,\,=\,\,\Big\{\hskip 1.00006pt{(\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I},\mu^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}I})\in\mathds{P}_{16}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}\,\Big|\,\hskip 0.50003pt\phi_{1}=0}\,\Big\}\hskip 0.50003pt\Big/\hskip 1.00006pt\mathrm{U}(1)\,,

(76)

which is free from
both the rephasing and shift redundancies
identified earlier.
A particular slice on the
\mathrm{U}(1)
orbit may be chosen in terms of the gauge-fixing function

\displaystyle\chi^{1}\,=\,\frac{1}{2i}\hskip 1.00006pt\log\bigg(\,{\frac{\det(\lambda)}{\det(\bar{\lambda})}}\,\bigg)\,.

(77)

Consequently, the following series of reductions arises for the massive twistor model:

\displaystyle\mathds{P}_{16}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}\,\supset\,\mathds{P}_{14}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}\,\supset\,\mathds{P}_{12}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}(m)\,.

(78)

Computation shows that the Dirac brackets between

(x^{\mu},y^{\mu},p_{\mu})
on
\mathds{P}_{14}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}

take the same form as the universal Poisson brackets in Eq. (
5
):
see Ref.
Kim:2024grz
.
Again, the Dirac brackets split into universal and model-specific parts:
see Eq. (
396
).
Similarly,
the Dirac brackets between

(x^{\mu},y^{\mu},p_{\mu})
on
\mathds{P}_{12}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}

take the same form as
Eq. (
1
).

By further reductions of the spin phase space
described in Sec.
4
,
one constructs submanifolds of
\mathds{P}_{14}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}
and
\mathds{P}_{12}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}(m)

providing explicit embeddings of the universal phase spaces:

\displaystyle\mathds{P}_{10}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}(w)\,\cong\,\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{10}(w)\,,\quad\mathds{P}_{8}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}(m,w)\,\cong\,\mathbf{P}\kern-0.02502pt_{8}(m,w)\,.

(79)

4
Hierarchy of Spin Phase Spaces

In Sec.
3
,
we have reviewed four spinning particle models
in Hamiltonian formulation
and clarified their constraints.
After appropriate removal of unphysical degrees of freedom,
the oscillator class of models
describes
12
-dimensional reduced phase spaces
while
the spherical top and massive twistor models
describe
14
-dimensional reduced phase spaces.
It can be seen that they are respectively isomorphic to

T^{*}\mathbb{M}\times T^{*}S^{2}

and

T^{*}\mathbb{M}\times T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)

up to double cover,
which describe
spin (rotational) phase spaces

T^{*}S^{2}
and
T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)
.

In this section,
we establish a hierarchy of spin phase spaces:

\displaystyle T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)\,\supset\,T^{*}S^{2}\,\supset\,S^{2}\,.

(80)

Via double cover, it also holds that

T^{*}\mathrm{SU}(2)\supset\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\times}\supset\mathbb{CP}^{1}\cong S^{2}
.
This establishes that
all of the four models
are physically equivalent up to symplectic quotients,
in the free theory.
In the interacting theory,
this fact will imply that
the physics of the four models
are the same
as long as their interactions with external fields
are compatible with the quotients.

4.1
Definitions

For the sake of clarity,
we shall start by stating the definitions of each spin phase space in Eq. (
80
).
It suffices to work in the nonrelativistic notation.

\bm{T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)}

The six-dimensional spin phase space
T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)

is the cotangent bundle of the group manifold
\mathrm{SO}(3)
.
It is coordinatized by

\displaystyle\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\Lambda^{i}{}_{a},\hskip 1.00006ptS_{i}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,,

(81)

where the indices
i
and
a

run through
\{1,2,3\}
.
The group element
\Lambda^{i}{}_{a}\in\mathrm{SO}(3)

encodes the three Eulerian angles.
The fiber coordinates
S_{i}

describe the spin angular momentum as a pseudovector.
Physically, this phase space describes
the nonrelativistic spherical top
with fixed center.
The Poisson structure is given by

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\Lambda^{i}{}_{a}},{\Lambda^{j}{}_{b}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{6}\,=\,0\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\Lambda^{i}{}_{a}},{S_{k}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{6}\,=\,\varepsilon^{i}{}_{kj}\,\Lambda^{j}{}_{a}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{S_{i}},{S_{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{6}\,=\,\varepsilon^{k}{}_{ij}\hskip 1.00006ptS_{k}\,.

(82)

There are two
\mathrm{SO}(3)
symmetries acting on this phase space:
the left symmetry that rotates the external index
i

and
the right symmetry that rotates the internal index
a
.
The Poisson brackets in Eq. (
82
)
are invariant under these symmetry actions.

\bm{T^{*}S^{2}}

The four-dimensional spin phase space
T^{*}S^{2}

is the cotangent bundle of the two-sphere
S^{2}
.
It is coordinatized by

(\theta,\phi,p_{\theta},p_{\phi})
,
where
(\theta,\phi)
coordinatizes the base
S^{2}
.
The Poisson structure is given such that

(p_{\theta},p_{\phi})
are canonically conjugate
to
(\theta,\phi)
.
5
5

5

A discussion of
T^{*}S^{2}
as a spin phase space
may be an uncommon practice in the literature.
For instance, Ref.
Pauli:1927qhd
briefly introduces four variables
(s,\chi,s_{z},\varphi)

but then quickly discards
(s,\chi)
to descend to
S^{2}
.
A similar approach is taken by Ref.
Kuzenko:1994ju
as well.

Physically,
T^{*}S^{2}
is the phase space of
a familiar example in constrained Hamiltonian mechanics:
particle on a sphere.
A point particle in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
describes the canonical brackets

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{U^{i}},{U^{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{U^{i}},{V^{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\delta^{ij}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{V^{i}},{V^{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0\,,

(83)

where
\vec{U}
and
\vec{V}

denote the position and momentum, respectively.
The set of constraints that restricts this particle on
a sphere
S^{2}
embedded in the three-space
is given by

\displaystyle\vec{U}^{2}-\rho^{2}\,=\,0\,,\quad\vec{V}{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\vec{U}\,=\,0\,.

(84)

Here, the radius of the sphere is characterized by a dimensionful constant
\rho

while
\vec{V}{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\vec{U}=0
ensures the tangency of the particle’s momentum to the sphere.
Imposing the constraints in Eq. (
84
)
derives the Dirac bracket,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{U^{i}},{U^{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{4}\,&=\,0\,,\\[0.60004pt]
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{U^{i}},{V^{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{4}\,&=\,\delta^{ij}-\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\,U^{i}\hskip 0.50003ptU^{j}\,,\\
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{V^{i}},{V^{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{4}\,&=\,-\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\,\Big(\,{U^{i}\hskip 0.50003ptV^{j}-V^{i}\hskip 0.50003ptU^{j}}\,\Big)\,.\end{split}

(85)

It can be shown that
Eq. (
85
) precisely describes the canonical Poisson structure on
T^{*}S^{2}
.
Mathematically, this establishes that

T^{*}S^{2}
is embedded in
T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
The explicit embedding map
describes
U^{i}=\rho\hskip 1.00006pt(\sin\theta\cos\phi,\sin\theta\sin\phi,\cos\theta)
,
for instance.

Notably,
there exists an alternative embedding of

T^{*}S^{2}
in
T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Suppose three-vector variables
\vec{u},\vec{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}

that satisfy

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{u^{i}},{u^{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{u^{i}},{v^{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\delta^{ij}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{v^{i}},{v^{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0\,.

(86)

To clarify,
this posits a new
T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{3}

that is independent from the earlier
T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{3}
in Eq. (
83
).
With this understanding, suppose constraints

\displaystyle\vec{u}^{2}-\vec{v}^{2}\,=\,0\,,\quad\vec{u}{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\vec{v}\,=\,0\,.

(87)

The resulting Dirac bracket describes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{u^{i}},{u^{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{4}\,&=\,-\frac{1}{2|\vec{u}||\vec{v}|}\,\Big(\,{u^{i}\hskip 0.50003ptv^{j}-v^{i}\hskip 0.50003ptu^{j}}\,\Big)\,=\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{v^{i}},{v^{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{4}\,,\\
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{u^{i}},{v^{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{4}\,&=\,\delta^{ij}-\frac{1}{2|\vec{u}||\vec{v}|}\,\Big(\,{u^{i}\hskip 0.50003ptu^{j}+v^{i}\hskip 0.50003ptv^{j}}\,\Big)\,,\end{split}

(88)

which describes the Poisson structure of a four-dimensional submanifold.
It turns out that this submanifold is exactly
T^{*}S^{2}
,
as Eq. (
88
) precisely reproduces Eq. (
85
)
via

\displaystyle\vec{u}\,=\,\frac{\rho}{|\vec{U}|}\,\vec{U}\,,\quad\vec{v}\,=\,\frac{|\vec{U}|}{\rho}\,\vec{V}{\quad\implies\quad}\vec{u}\times\vec{v}\,=\,\vec{U}\times\vec{V}\,.

(89)

In this light,
the constant
\rho
would be considered spurious:
it completely disappears from the formulation in Eq. (
88
).

Physically, this alternative embedding of
T^{*}S^{2}

in
T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{3}

arises from the nonrelativistic vector oscillator model.
The constraints in
Eq. (
87
)
correspond to

(\Psi_{2},\bar{\Psi}_{2})

in Eq. (
28
).

\bm{S^{2}}

The two-dimensional spin phase space
S^{2}

is the two-sphere with coordinates
(\theta,\phi)

in which the area element,
oriented as
\omega=w\hskip 1.00006pt\sin\theta\,d\phi{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}d\theta
,
serves as the symplectic form

shankar2012principles
.
This means to have the Poisson bracket

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\theta},{\phi}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{2}\,=\,\frac{1}{w\hskip 1.00006pt\sin\theta}\,.

(90)

Alternatively,
the two-dimensional spin phase space
S^{2}

can be realized as a coadjoint orbit

kirillov1975elements
;
kostant1970orbits
;
souriau1970structure
;
woodhouse1997geometric
;
kirillov2004lectures
.
Consider the three-dimensional Poisson manifold
\mathbb{R}^{3}\hskip-0.24994pt\hskip-1.00006pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt\hskip-1.00006pt\{0\}
,
whose coordinates
S_{i}
satisfy the Poisson bracket relation

\{\hskip 0.50003pt{S_{i}},{S_{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}=\varepsilon^{k}{}_{ij}\hskip 1.00006ptS_{k}
.
The rank of
\varepsilon^{k}{}_{ij}\hskip 1.00006ptS_{k}

as an antisymmetric matrix,
described by row and column indices
i
and
j
,
is
2
.
Explicitly, the Casimir
\vec{S}^{2}
Poisson-commutes with any function of
\vec{S}
.
Thus, the symplectic leaves are characterized by the equation

\displaystyle\vec{S}^{2}-w^{2}\,=\,0\,,

(91)

where
w>0
is a constant.
The symplectic leaf characterized by Eq. (
91
)
is a two-sphere
S^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
of radius
w

and exhibits the Poisson structure

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{S_{i}},{S_{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{2}\,=\,\varepsilon^{k}{}_{ij}\hskip 1.00006ptS_{k}\,.

(92)

The above two definitions are equivalent.
The explicit embedding
S^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
is

\displaystyle S_{i}\,=\,w\hskip 1.00006pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\sin\theta\cos\phi,\hskip 1.00006pt\sin\theta\sin\phi,\hskip 1.00006pt\cos\theta}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,.

(93)

Combining
Eqs. (
90
) and (
93
)
reproduces Eq. (
92
).

4.2
Successive Reductions

Provided the characterizations of the spin phase spaces in Sec.
4.1
,
we can now readily establish their hierarchy
via successive implementations of symplectic reduction.

From 6 to 4

First, we establish the reduction from
the six-dimensional

T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)
to
the four-dimensional

T^{*}S^{2}
.
This reduction is relevant for the spherical top and massive twistor models in Secs.
3.3
and
3.4
.
It suffices to work in the nonrelativistic notation.

Take the definition of
T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)

by Eq. (
82
).
Let
R^{a}{}_{b}
be an arbitrary constant
\mathrm{SO}(3)
matrix.
Imposing
S_{i}\,\Lambda^{i}{}_{a}\hskip 1.00006ptR^{a}{}_{1}{\>=\>}0{\>=\>}S_{i}\,\Lambda^{i}{}_{a}\hskip 1.00006ptR^{a}{}_{2}

makes
\Lambda^{i}{}_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptR^{a}{}_{3}
parallel to
S^{i}
.
Without loss of generality,
we can perform a right symmetry transformation

\Lambda^{i}{}_{a}\mapsto\Lambda^{i}{}_{b}\hskip 1.00006pt(R^{-1})^{b}{}_{a}
,

S_{i}\mapsto S_{i}

on
T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)

to have

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}\,=\,S_{i}\,\Lambda^{i}{}_{1}\,,\quad\Psi_{2}\,=\,S_{i}\,\Lambda^{i}{}_{2}\,.

(94)

Computation shows that the Dirac bracket
setting
\Psi_{1}=0=\Psi_{2}

is given by

\displaystyle{\begin{array}[]{lll}\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\Lambda^{i}{}_{1}},{\Lambda^{j}{}_{2}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{4}\,=\,\frac{\Lambda^{i}{}_{3}\hskip 1.00006pt\Lambda^{j}{}_{3}}{|\vec{S}|}&\,,\quad\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\Lambda^{i}{}_{1}},{\Lambda^{j}{}_{3}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{4}\,=\,-\frac{\Lambda^{i}{}_{3}\hskip 1.00006pt\Lambda^{j}{}_{2}}{|\vec{S}|}&\,,\quad\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\Lambda^{i}{}_{a}},{S_{k}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{4}\,=\,\varepsilon^{i}{}_{kj}\,\Lambda^{j}{}_{a}\,,\\
\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\Lambda^{i}{}_{2}},{\Lambda^{j}{}_{3}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{4}\,=\,\frac{\Lambda^{i}{}_{3}\hskip 0.50003pt\Lambda^{j}{}_{1}}{|\vec{S}|}&\,,\quad\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\Lambda^{i}{}_{3}},{\Lambda^{j}{}_{3}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{4}\,=\,\frac{\varepsilon^{ij}{}_{k}\hskip 1.00006pt\Lambda^{k}{}_{3}}{|\vec{S}|}&\,,\quad\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{S_{i}},{S_{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{4}\,=\,\varepsilon^{k}{}_{ij}\,S_{k}\,.\end{array}}

The four-dimensional constrained phase space which Eq. (
4.2
) describes is
T^{*}S^{2}
.
The explicit embedding

T^{*}S^{2}\to T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)

is given by

\displaystyle\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\Lambda^{i}{}_{1},\hskip 1.00006pt\Lambda^{i}{}_{2},\hskip 1.00006pt\Lambda^{i}{}_{3}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,=\,\bigg(\,{\frac{u^{i}}{|\vec{u}|},\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{v^{i}}{|\vec{v}|},\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{\varepsilon^{i}{}_{jk}\hskip 0.50003ptu^{j}v^{k}}{|\vec{u}||\vec{v}|}}\,\bigg)\,,\quad S^{i}\,=\,\varepsilon^{i}{}_{jk}\hskip 0.50003ptu^{j}v^{k}\,.

(97)

Eq. (
88
) implies Eq. (
4.2
)
via Eq. (
97
).

From 4 to 2

Next, we establish the reduction from the four-dimensional
T^{*}S^{2}
to the two-dimensional
S^{2}
.
This reduction is relevant for all models
in our setup,
as
T^{*}S^{2}
serves as a bridge between
T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)
and
S^{2}
.

Take the definition of
T^{*}S^{2}
by Eq. (
88
).
Suppose constraints

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}\,=\,|\vec{u}||\vec{v}|-w\,,\quad\Psi_{4}\,=\,\tan^{-1}\hskip-1.00006pt\bigg(\,{\frac{\vec{\eta}{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\vec{v}}{\vec{\eta}{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\vec{u}}}\,\bigg)\,,

(98)

where
\vec{\eta}
is a constant reference.
These are the nonrelativistic analogs of

(\Psi_{3},\Psi_{4})

in Eq. (
28
).

\Psi_{3}
generates
\mathrm{SO}(2)
rotations on the two-plane
perpendicular to
\vec{S}=\vec{u}\times\vec{v}

while fixing
|\vec{S}|
;

\Psi_{4}
is the angle such that

\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\Psi_{3}},{\Psi_{4}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{4}=-1
.
The resulting Dirac bracket
exhibits dependencies in
\vec{\eta}

and may describe subtleties
regarding topological obstructions
in promoting
\Psi_{4}
to
a globally defined function.
Regardless,
S^{i}=\varepsilon^{i}{}_{jk}\hskip 1.00006ptu^{j}\hskip 0.50003ptv^{k}

serve as good coordinates on the two-dimensional constraint surface.
Their Dirac brackets are precisely Eq. (
92
)
since
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{S_{i}},{\Psi_{3}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}=0
,
establishing that
imposing Eq. (
98
)
on
T^{*}S^{2}

derives
S^{2}
.

From 6 to 2

Lastly, it should be clear that
a direct reduction from the six-dimensional
T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)
to the two-dimensional
S^{2}

is also viable,
in which case one imposes

(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\Psi_{3},\Psi_{4})

at once
with

(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})
in Eq. (
94
)
and

\Psi_{3}=|\vec{S}|-w
,

\Psi_{4}=\tan^{-1}(\eta_{i}\hskip 1.00006pt\Lambda^{i}{}_{2}/\eta_{j}\hskip 1.00006pt\Lambda^{j}{}_{1})
.

5
Universality in Interacting Theory: Electromagnetism

Through Secs.
2
and
3
,
we have explicitly established that
any model of a massive spinning particle
describes
physical variables

(x^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})

that exhibit the following
universal Poisson bracket relation
in the
free theory
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{x^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,\,&=\,\,\frac{1}{-p^{2}}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\hat{y}_{\rho}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\sigma}\,=\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\hat{y}^{\mu}},{\hat{y}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,,\\
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{\hat{y}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,\,&=\,\,\frac{1}{-p^{2}}\,\Big(\,{\hat{y}^{\mu}p^{\nu}+p^{\mu}\hat{y}^{\nu}}\,\Big)\,,\\[1.79993pt]
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,\,&=\,\,\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\,.\end{split}

(99)

Our objective now
is to
generalize such universal structures
in the
interacting theory
.
The output is model-agnostic derivations of
the physical EoM of massive spinning particles
coupled to electromagnetism and gravity.
For clarity of exposition, we first discuss electromagnetic interactions in this section and defer gravitational interactions to Sec.
6
.

5.1
Couplings as Symplectic Perturbations

Our point of departure is
the celebrated fact in symplectic geometry
that
a manifestly gauge-invariant coupling
of particles to electromagnetic fields
is viable by
modifying the symplectic structure
in terms of the field strength.
This construction is due to Souriau
souriau1970structure

and also traces back to Feynman
dyson1990feynman
.
Historically, this manifestly gauge-invariant method has served as an important milestone in the study of particle mechanics in the symplectic framework

woodhouse1997geometric
;
torrence1973gauge
;
guillemin1978equations
;
sternberg1978classical
;
guillemin1990symplectic
.
Unfortunately,
it appears to be less recognized in recent literature,
so we shall provide a brief review
(see also Ref.
csg
).

For the sake of concreteness,
let us suppose a simple example:
a massive scalar particle in Minkowski space.
This is a Hamiltonian system defined on
the cotangent bundle
T^{*}\mathbb{M}
.
In the free theory,
this phase space is
equipped with the symplectic form and Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\omega^{\circ}\,=\,dp_{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{\mu}\,,\quad\phi_{0}\,=\,\frac{1}{2m}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{p^{2}+m^{2}}\,\Big)\,.

(100)

We have started to put the accent
∘

to explicitly indicate free-theory objects.
The canonical Poisson bracket reads

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{x^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,0\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,0\,.

(101)

The Souriau-Feynman method of electromagnetic coupling
modifies the geometric data in Eq. (
100
) as

\displaystyle\omega\,=\,dp_{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{\mu}\,+\,qF\,,\quad\phi_{0}\,=\,\frac{1}{2m}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{p^{2}+m^{2}}\,\Big)\,,

(102)

where
F=\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006ptF_{\mu\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{\nu}
is the field strength two-form,
and
q
is the electric charge.
Crucially, the Hamiltonian is left unchanged
while the symplectic form is perturbed as

\displaystyle\omega\,=\,\omega^{\circ}\hskip 1.00006pt+\,\omega^{\prime}\,,

(103)

with
\omega^{\prime}=qF
.
As a result, the Poisson bracket in Eq. (
101
) is altered as

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{x^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,qF_{\mu\nu}(x)\,.

(104)

To see why Eq. (
102
) achieves the electromagnetic coupling,
one can derive the EoM:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{x}^{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt=\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{\phi_{0}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\hskip 1.00006pt=\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{p^{\mu}}{m}\,,\quad\dot{p}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt=\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{\mu}},{\phi_{0}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\hskip 1.00006pt=\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{q}{m}\,F_{\mu\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\nu}\,.\end{split}

(105)

Eq. (
105
) exactly describes
the EoM of a charged scalar particle in electromagnetic background.
The modified
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}
bracket in Eq. (
104
)
implements the Lorentz force law
in Eq. (
105
).
As a result, the intuition for noncanonical bracket
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\neq 0

is the cyclotron precession exhibited by the momentum
p_{\mu}

in the external electromagnetic field.

Crucially,
this derivation of the Lorentz force law
does not invoke the electromagnetic gauge potential at all,
as is emphasized by both
Souriau
souriau1970structure

and Feynman
dyson1990feynman
.
As a result,
p_{\mu}

is the gauge-invariant, physical momentum
(also known as kinetic momentum).

The only consistency requirement in this framework
is the closure of the modified symplectic form in Eq. (
102
)

souriau1970structure
,
which holds via the Bianchi-type field equations
dF=0
on the background
dyson1990feynman
.
Physically, this closure
amounts to the
Liouville property
of classical time evolution:
conservation of classical probability
(as phase space measure)
Kim:2025sey
;
csg
.

If one insists on keeping the canonical Poisson brackets,
one can perform
a gauge-dependent coordinate transformation
in the phase space
(a change of worldline field basis):

\displaystyle p_{\mu}\,=\,{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{p}}_{\mu}-qA_{\mu}(x)\,.

(106)

Here,
A{\>=\>}A_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}
is the electromagnetic gauge potential
such that
F{\>=\>}dA
.
As the symplectic form in Eq. (
102
)
is the exterior derivative of

p_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}+qA={\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{p}}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}
,
the geometric data in
Eq. (
102
) now appears as

\displaystyle\omega\,=\,d{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{p}}_{\mu}\wedge dx^{\mu}\,,\quad\phi_{0}\,=\,\frac{1}{2m}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{p}}^{2}+m^{2}}\,\Big)-\frac{q}{m}\hskip 1.00006ptA_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{p}}^{\mu}+\frac{q^{2}}{2m}\hskip 1.00006ptA_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptA^{\mu}(x)\,.

(107)

As a result, the Hamiltonian is perturbed and solely encodes the electromagnetic interactions,
while the symplectic structure is brought back to the free-theory form:

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{x^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{p}}_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{p}}_{\mu}},{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{p}}_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0\,.

(108)

This formulation, however, exhibits evident disadvantages
in both conceptual and practical aspects.
First, gauge invariance is grossly obscured.
The canonical momentum
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{p}}_{\mu}
is not a gauge-invariant observable.
Second, the coupling is nonlinear.
In perturbation theory,
the
(q^{2}/2)\hskip 1.00006ptA_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptA^{\mu}(x)
term in Eq. (
107
)
translates to the spurious quartic vertex in scalar quantum electrodynamics.
Third, the derivation of the Lorentz force law
from the Hamiltonian EoM
becomes not so simple.

In this paper,
we always fix the Hamiltonian in the free-theory form
such that the external field couplings are
implemented solely by modifications of the symplectic structure
(declaration of worldline field basis).
When the symplectic form is modified in the form of Eq. (
103
),
we say that the two-form
\omega^{\prime}

is a
symplectic perturbation
.

We end with a useful formula.
The pointwise inverses of
\omega^{\circ}
and
\omega

define
the Poisson bivectors

\Pi^{\circ}
and
\Pi

of the free and interacting theories,
respectively.
Their relation is

\displaystyle\omega\,=\,\omega^{\circ}\hskip 1.00006pt+\,\omega^{\prime}{\quad\implies\quad}\Pi\,=\,\Pi^{\circ}-\Pi^{\circ}\hskip 1.00006pt\omega^{\prime}\,\Pi\,.

(109)

In writing down Eq. (
109
), we have treated

\omega
,
\omega^{\circ}
,
\omega^{\prime}
,
\Pi
,
\Pi^{\circ}

like antisymmetric matrices.

5.2
Model-Agnostic Derivation of BMT Equations

By combining the ideas in Secs.
2
and
5.1
,
we establish
universal recipes
of coupling masssive spinning particles to electromagnetism.

Universal Recipe for Minimal Coupling

Suppose a free massive spinning particle
formulated in a symplectic manifold
\mathds{P}
.
The reader may pick any of the explicit examples in Sec.
3
.
Let
\omega^{\circ}
be the symplectic form given to
\mathds{P}
.
Time evolution describes a Hamiltonian vector field on
\mathds{P}
,
which we denote as
T^{\circ}
.

The result in Sec.
2.2

implies that
there exist functions
(x^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})

on
\mathds{P}

that satisfy the universal Poisson brackets in Eq. (
99
).
Without loss of generality, we assume that
these functions evolve under the free-theory time evolution as

\displaystyle T^{\circ}[\,{x^{\mu}}\,]\,=\,\frac{p^{\mu}}{m}\,,\quad T^{\circ}[\,{\hat{y}^{\mu}}\,]\,=\,0\,,\quad T^{\circ}[\,{p_{\mu}}\,]\,=\,0\,.

(110)

Physically, this means that
the free particle follows a straight-line trajectory
while carrying a constant spin pseudovector.

As an application of the Souriau-Feynman framework in Sec.
5.1
,
we suppose the following symplectic perturbation on
\mathds{P}
:

\displaystyle\omega^{\prime}\,\,=\,\,qF\,\,=\,\,\frac{1}{2}\,qF_{\rho\sigma}(x)\,dx^{\rho}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{\sigma}\,.

(111)

The formula in Eq. (
109
) then implies that
the Poisson bivector is modified as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Pi(df,dg)\,=\,\Pi^{\circ}(df,dg)\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 1.00006pt\Pi^{\circ}(df,dx^{\rho})\,qF_{\rho\sigma}(x)\,\Pi(dx^{\sigma},dg)\,.\end{split}

(112)

Iterating Eq. (
112
) will provide an infinite series expansion for
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}=\Pi(df,dg)
,
which governs how the Poisson bracket is modified under the symplectic perturbation:

\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}=\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{x^{\rho}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,qF_{\rho\sigma}(x)\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\sigma}},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{x^{\rho}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,qF_{\rho\sigma}(x)\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\sigma}},{x^{\kappa}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,qF_{\kappa\lambda}(x)\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\lambda}},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 1.00006pt\cdots
.

Let
T
be the time-evolution Hamiltonian vector field
in the interacting theory
such that the Hamiltonian EoM reads
\dot{f}=T[\,{f}\,]
.
Eqs. (
110
) and (
112
) together implies that

\displaystyle\dot{f}\,=\,T^{\circ}[\,{f}\,]\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{x^{\rho}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,qF_{\rho\sigma}(x)\,\dot{x}^{\sigma}\,.

(113)

Plugging in the physical variables
(x^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})
to
f
in Eq. (
113
),
we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{x}^{\mu}\,&=\,\frac{p^{\mu}}{m}\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{1}{m^{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\varepsilon^{\mu\rho\kappa\lambda}\hat{y}_{\kappa}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\lambda}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{qF_{\rho\sigma}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\sigma}}\,\Big)\,,\\
\dot{\hat{y}}^{\mu}\,&=\,\frac{1}{m^{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\hat{y}^{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\rho}+p^{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}^{\rho}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{qF_{\rho\sigma}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\sigma}}\,\Big)\,,\\[1.43994pt]
\dot{p}^{\mu}\,&=\,qF^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\nu}\,,\end{split}

(114)

where we have used Eq. (
99
)
to evaluate

\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{x^{\rho}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}
,

\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\hat{y}^{\mu}},{x^{\rho}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}
,
and

\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{\mu}},{x^{\rho}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}
.

Eq. (
114
) defines the classical EoM of a consistent Hamiltonian system,
as the modified symplectic form due to Eq. (
111
) is closed.
What is its physical interpretation, then?

BMT Equations

The BMT
Bargmann:1959gz
equations
dictate the dynamics of charged massive spinning particles in external electromagnetic fields
at the linear order in electric charge
q
,
in the regime where derivative effects can be completely ignored:

F(x)\gg\hat{y}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial F(x),\hat{y}^{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial^{2}F(x),\cdots
.
In our conventions, they are

\displaystyle\dot{x}^{\mu}\,

\displaystyle=\,\frac{p^{\mu}}{m}\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{q}{m}\,\bigg[\,{\frac{g}{2}\,{*}\hskip-0.50003ptF^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(x)+\bigg(\,{1-\frac{g}{2}}\,\bigg)\,\hat{\delta}^{\mu}{}_{\kappa}\,{*}\hskip-0.50003ptF^{\kappa}{}_{\nu}(x)}\,\bigg]\,\hat{y}^{\nu}+\cdots\,,

(115a)

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{y}}^{\mu}\,

\displaystyle=\,\frac{q}{m}\,\bigg[\,{F^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(x)-\bigg(\,{1-\frac{g}{2}}\,\bigg)\,\hat{\delta}^{\mu}{}_{\kappa}\hskip 1.00006ptF^{\kappa}{}_{\nu}(x)}\,\bigg]\,\hat{y}^{\nu}+\cdots\,,

(115b)

\displaystyle\dot{p}^{\mu}\,

\displaystyle=\,\frac{q}{m}\,F^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\nu}+\cdots\,,

(115c)

where the ellipses signify that
terms of
\mathcal{O}(q^{1+n})
or
\mathcal{O}(\partial^{n}\hskip-1.00006ptF)

are discarded from the right-hand sides
for
n\geq 1
.
See App.
C.1
for more details.

Many references present just Eqs. (
115b
) and (
115c
)
without explicitly stating the relation between
p^{\mu}
and
\dot{x}^{\mu}
.
However, it should be emphasized that
Eq. (
115a
)
must be specified
together with Eqs. (
115b
) and (
115c
)
to fully define the time evolution
and ensure its consistency,
especially when working in the Hamiltonian formulation.

In Eq. (
115
), the constant parameter
g

is the gyromagnetic ratio,
also known as the
g
-factor.
The gyromagnetic ratio controls the spin-induced magnetic dipole moment.
The specific value
g{\>=\>}0

has been identified as the minimal coupling
in the traditional sense,
in which case
the BMT equations in Eq. (
115
) read

\displaystyle\dot{x}^{\mu}\,

\displaystyle=\,\frac{p^{\mu}}{m}\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{q}{m}\,\hat{\delta}^{\mu}{}_{\kappa}\hskip 1.00006pt{*}\hskip-0.50003ptF^{\kappa}{}_{\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}^{\nu}+\cdots\,,

(116a)

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{y}}^{\mu}\,

\displaystyle=\,-\frac{q}{m}\,\hat{p}^{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{p}_{\kappa}\hskip 0.24994ptF^{\kappa}{}_{\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}^{\nu}+\cdots\,,

(116b)

\displaystyle\dot{p}^{\mu}\,

\displaystyle=\,\frac{q}{m}\,F^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\nu}+\cdots\,.

(116c)

Notably,
the EoM derived in Eq. (
114
)
yield the
g{\>=\>}0
BMT equations in Eq. (
116
)
through
plugging in
\dot{x}^{\mu}=p^{\mu}\hskip-1.00006pt/m+\mathcal{O}(q^{1})

and using an identity involving two epsilon tensors.
Therefore,
the
g{\>=\>}0
BMT equations
arise in any massive spinning particle model
by prescribing
the symplectic perturbation
in Eq. (
111
)
realized at the physical center
x^{\mu}
.

Universal Recipe for Dipolar Coupling

To implement the dipolar coupling in our universal framework,
consider the following symplectic perturbation:

\displaystyle\omega^{\prime}\,\,=\,\,\frac{1}{2}\,qF_{\mu\nu}(x)\,dx^{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{\nu}\,+\,q\hskip 1.00006ptd\,\bigg(\,{c_{1}\,\hat{y}^{\rho}\,{*}F_{\rho\sigma}(x)\,dx^{\sigma}}\,\bigg)\,.

(117)

Here,
c_{1}
is a constant.
Eq. (
117
) is the most general symplectic perturbation
that can contribute to the
\mathcal{O}(q^{1}\hat{y}^{1})
part of the EoM.
It is
bootstrapped
by
the physical principles of

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{(a)}\,\,&\text{
Liouville property}\,,\\
\text{(b)}\,\,&\text{
Poincar\'{e} invariance}\,,\\
\text{(c)}\,\,&\text{
Gauge invariance}\,,\\
\text{(d)}\,\,&\text{
Parity invariance}\,.\end{split}

(118)

By applying the method demonstrated in Eq. (
113
),
one finds that
the
\mathcal{O}(q^{1}y^{1})
contribution to the resulting EoM
arises from the combination

\displaystyle-\frac{q}{m}\,\bigg[\,{\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{x^{\rho}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,F_{\rho\sigma}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\sigma}+c_{1}\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{\hat{y}^{\rho}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,{*}F_{\rho\sigma}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\sigma}}\,\bigg]\,.

(119)

By examining Eq. (
119
) for
f=(x^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})
,
one exactly reproduces the BMT equations in Eq. (
115
)
provided the identification

\displaystyle c_{1}\,=\,g/2\,.

(120)

Therefore, the BMT equations with the generic gyromagnetic ratio
g

arise in any massive spinning particle model
by prescribing the symplectic perturbation in Eq. (
117
)
in terms of
the physical center
x^{\mu}
and spin length pseudovector
\hat{y}^{\mu}
.

5.3
The Four Models with Electromagnetic Couplings

It remains to substantiate and reproduce
the universal derivation of BMT equations
in Sec.
5.2

from each of our four example models.

To be fully instructive,
this subsection wishes to
derive the EoM
by varying first-order actions
with Lagrange multipliers,
instead of retaining the sophisticated geometrical languages of symplectic forms and Dirac brackets.
Our first-order actions
take the form

\displaystyle\int\,\bigg[\,{\,\theta\,-\,\kappa^{0}\hskip 1.00006pt\phi_{0}\,d\tau\,-\,\Big(\,{\text{extra constraint terms}}\,\Big)\,d\tau\hskip 1.00006pt}\,\bigg]\,,

(121)

which defines sigma models from
\mathbb{R}
to
phase spaces with constraints.
Here,

\tau
is the worldline parameter,

\theta
is (the pullback of) the symplectic potential,

\phi_{0}
is the mass-shell constraint,
and
\kappa^{0}
is a Lagrange multiplier.
Furthermore,
there will be extra constraints
so that
more Lagrange multipliers are employed,
except for the spinor oscillator model.
We start from the spinorial models,
as they do not involve the intricacies regarding the spurious center.

Spinor Oscillator

The special-relativistic spinor oscillator
is a sigma model
\mathbb{R}{\>\to\>}\mathds{P}^{\mathrm{SO}}_{12}
,
whose target space is
coordinatized by
(x^{\mu},p_{\mu},\zeta_{\alpha})
.

The electromagnetic minimal coupling
is implemented by the action

\displaystyle\int\,\bigg[\,{\,p_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2i}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\zeta}_{\dot{{\alpha}}}\,\hat{p}^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}\dot{\zeta}_{\alpha}-\dot{\bar{\zeta}}_{\dot{{\alpha}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{p}^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}\hskip 0.50003pt\zeta_{\alpha}}\,\Big)+qA_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\mu}-\frac{\kappa^{0}}{2m}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{p^{2}+m^{2}}\,\Big)}\,\bigg]\,d\tau\,,

(122)

which is a functional of

(x^{\mu},p_{\mu},\zeta_{\alpha})
and
\kappa^{0}
.
It is easy that
the symplectic potential due to Eq. (
122
) reproduces
the symplectic form in Eq. (
111
).
Variations of Eq. (
122
)
give

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{x}^{\mu}\,&=\,\frac{\kappa^{0}\hskip 0.50003ptp^{\mu}}{m}-\frac{q}{m^{2}}\,S^{\mu\rho}\hskip 1.00006ptF_{\rho\sigma}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\sigma}\,,\\
\dot{p}_{\mu}\,&=\,qF_{\mu\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\nu}\,,\\
\dot{\zeta}_{\alpha}\,&=\,-\frac{q}{2m^{2}}\,F_{\smash{\rho\sigma}\vphantom{{\beta}}}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\sigma}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\nu}\hskip 1.00006pt(\sigma^{\rho\nu})_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}\hskip 1.00006pt\zeta_{\beta}\,,\end{split}

(123)

and
p^{2}+m^{2}=0
.
Here,
S^{\mu\nu}:=\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\hat{y}_{\rho}\hskip 0.50003ptp_{\sigma}
.

The worldline parametrization can be fixed as
\kappa^{0}=1
.
Then Eq. (
123
) readily reproduces the
g=0
BMT equations.
In particular, the spin precession is governed by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{\zeta}_{\alpha}\,&=\,\frac{q}{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\bigg(\,{\bar{F}_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}(x)+\hat{p}_{{\alpha}{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\,F^{\dot{{\alpha}}}{}_{\dot{{\beta}}}(x)\,\hat{p}^{{\dot{{\beta}}}{\beta}}}\,\bigg)\hskip 1.00006pt\zeta_{\beta}+\mathcal{O}(q^{2})\,.\end{split}

(124)

Similarly, the BMT equations with generic
g
-factor
arise by
adding
(g/2)\hskip 1.00006ptq\hskip 1.00006pt{*}\hskip-0.50003ptF_{\mu\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}^{\mu}\hskip 0.24994pt\dot{x}^{\nu}

in the Lagrangian in Eq. (
122
)
with the definition of
\hat{y}^{\mu}
in Eq. (
42
).
In this case,
one finds that
the spin precession is governed by

\dot{\zeta}_{\alpha}=(\Omega_{\smash{\text{BMT}}})_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}\hskip 1.00006pt\zeta_{\beta}

with
ambikerr1

\displaystyle(\Omega_{\smash{\text{BMT}}})_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}\,=\,\frac{q}{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\bigg(\,{\frac{1{\,+\,}g/2}{2}\,\bar{F}_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}(x)+\frac{1{\,-\,}g/2}{2}\,\hat{p}_{{\alpha}{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\,F^{\dot{{\alpha}}}{}_{\dot{{\beta}}}(x)\,\hat{p}^{{\dot{{\beta}}}{\beta}}}\,\bigg)\,.

(125)

Massive Twistor

The special-relativistic massive twistor
is a sigma model
\mathbb{R}{\>\to\>}\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{MT}}}_{16}
,
whose target space can be
coordinatized by
(x^{\mu},y^{\mu},\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I})
.

The electromagnetic minimal coupling
is implemented by the action

\displaystyle\int\,\left[\,{\begin{aligned} &{-\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\,\dot{x}^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}}+i\hskip 1.00006pty^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\hskip 0.50003pt\dot{\bar{\lambda}}_{I{\dot{{\alpha}}}}{\,-\,}\dot{\lambda}_{\alpha}{}^{I}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{{\alpha}}}}}\,\Big)+qA_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\mu}\\
&-\frac{\kappa^{0}}{2m}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{m^{2}-\det(\lambda)\hskip 1.00006pt\det(\bar{\lambda})\hskip-0.50003pt}\,\Big)-\kappa^{1}\,\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\hskip 1.00006pty^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}\hskip 0.50003pt\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\end{aligned}}\,\right]\,d\tau\,,

(126)

which is a functional of

(x^{\mu},y^{\mu},\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I})
, and
(\kappa^{0},\kappa^{1})
.
It is easy that the symplectic potential due to Eq. (
126
) reproduces
the symplectic form in Eq. (
111
).
Variations of Eq. (
126
) give

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{x}^{\mu}\,&=\,\frac{\kappa^{0}\hskip 0.50003ptp^{\mu}}{m}-\frac{q}{m^{2}}\,\Big(\,{\varepsilon^{\mu\rho}{}_{\kappa\lambda}\hskip 1.00006pty^{\kappa}p^{\lambda}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006ptF_{\rho\sigma}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\sigma}\,,\\
\dot{y}^{\mu}\,&=\,\frac{q}{m^{2}}\,\Big(\,{y^{\mu}p^{\rho}+p^{\mu}y^{\rho}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006ptF_{\rho\sigma}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\sigma}\,,\\
\dot{\lambda}_{\alpha}{}^{I}\,&=\,-\frac{1}{2}\,(\sigma^{\rho})_{{\alpha}{\dot{{\alpha}}}}\hskip 1.00006pt(\bar{\lambda}^{-1})^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}I}\hskip 1.00006ptqF_{\rho\sigma}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\sigma}-\frac{i\hskip 0.24994pt\kappa^{1}}{2}\,\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\,,\end{split}

(127)

together with
p^{2}+m^{2}=0
and
p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}y=0
.

The Lagrange multiplier
\kappa^{1}

will be fixed by imposing
an explicit gauge-fixing condition
such as the one in Eq. (
77
).
Physical observables are nonetheless
\mathrm{U}(1)
invariant,
so their time evolution is uniquely determined
even if
\kappa^{1}
is left agnostic.
With this understanding,
it is easy to reproduce the
g{\>=\>}0
BMT equations
from Eq. (
127
).

The massive twistor model exhibits some nice features.
First of all,
the universal derivation in Sec.
5.2

reincarnates
in an almost identical fashion
when obtaining
the first two lines in Eq. (
127
),
as
the model naturally features the variables

x^{\mu}
and
y^{\mu}
.
Second of all,
the EoM of the spin frame
\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}

encapsulate the EoM of both the momentum and spin,
thanks to the fact that
the body-frame components of spin
are dynamically conserved:

\hat{y}^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}\propto(\bar{\lambda}^{-1})^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}I}\hskip 1.00006pt(\vec{W}{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\vec{\sigma})_{I}{}^{J}\hskip 1.00006pt(\lambda^{-1})_{J}{}^{\alpha}

for some constant
\vec{W}
.

Similarly, it is easy to derive the BMT equations with generic
g
,
in which case
the spin frame’s EoM are

\dot{\lambda}_{\alpha}{}^{I}=(\Omega_{\smash{\text{BMT}}})_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}\hskip 1.00006pt\lambda_{\beta}{}^{I}-(i\kappa^{1}/2)\hskip 1.00006pt\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}

with
(\Omega_{\smash{\text{BMT}}})_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}
given in Eq. (
125
).

Vector Oscillator

The special-relativistic vector oscillator
is a sigma model
\mathbb{R}{\>\to\>}\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}_{16}
,
whose target space
\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}_{16}
is
coordinatized by
({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu},p_{\mu},{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu})
.
The reduced phase space
\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}_{12}

with no unphysical spinning degrees of freedom
arises by imposing four spin constraints.

The electromagnetic minimal coupling is implemented by the action

\displaystyle\int\,\left[\,{\,\begin{aligned} &p_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}}^{\mu}+\frac{i}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}}^{\mu}-\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}}\,\Big)\\
&+qA_{\mu}\hskip-0.50003pt\hskip-0.24994pt\bigg(\,{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}+i\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}})p}{p^{2}}}\,\bigg)\,\frac{d}{d\tau}\bigg(\,{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}+i\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}})^{\mu\nu}p_{\nu}}{p^{2}}}\,\bigg)\\
&-\frac{\kappa^{0}}{2m}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{p^{2}+m^{2}}\,\Big)-\kappa^{1}\hskip 1.00006ptp{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}-\bar{\kappa}^{1}\hskip 1.00006ptp{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}-\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{2}-\frac{\bar{\kappa}^{2}}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}^{2}\end{aligned}\hskip 1.00006pt}\,\right]\,d\tau\,,

(128)

which is a functional of
({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu},p_{\mu},{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu})
and
(\kappa^{0},\kappa^{1},\kappa^{2})
.
The complex Lagrange multipliers
\kappa^{1}
and
\kappa^{2}

impose the four necessary spin constraints,

p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}{\>=\>}0{\>=\>}p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}

and

{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{2}{\>=\>}0{\>=\>}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}^{2}
.

It should be clear that
Eq. (
128
)
is uniquely determined by
Eqs. (
111
) and (
27
).
Unfortunately, it describes a bulky formula
due to the very mismatch between
x
and
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}
.
However, two simplified formulations are viable.

The first approach
is based on the observation that
the constraints

p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}{\>=\>}0{\>=\>}p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}

are being explicitly imposed by Lagrange multipliers
in Eq. (
128
),
on the support of which
the spurious and physical centers coincide.
Thus, an equivalent, simplified action is

\displaystyle\int\,\left[\,{\,\begin{aligned} &p_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}}^{\mu}+\frac{i}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}}^{\mu}-\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\mu}}\,\Big)+qA_{\mu}({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}})\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}}^{\mu}\\
&-\frac{\kappa^{0}}{2m}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{p^{2}+m^{2}}\,\Big)-\kappa^{1}\hskip 1.00006ptp{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}-\bar{\kappa}^{1}\hskip 1.00006ptp{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}-\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{2}-\frac{\bar{\kappa}^{2}}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}^{2}\end{aligned}\hskip 1.00006pt}\,\right]\,d\tau\,.

(129)

In this particular sense,
the vector oscillator model
happens to allow for
realizing the interaction at the unphysical center
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}
,

at the level of action
.

Computation shows that
the saddle of either Eqs. (
128
) and (
129
) describes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}}^{\mu}\,&=\,\frac{p^{\mu}}{m}-\frac{i\hskip 0.50003pt({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}})^{\mu\rho}}{m^{2}}\hskip 1.00006ptqF_{\rho\sigma}({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}})\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}}^{\sigma}\,,\\
\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}}^{\mu}\,&=\,-\bigg(\,{\frac{p^{\mu}\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}}^{\rho}}{m^{2}}\hskip 1.00006ptqF_{\rho\sigma}({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}})\hskip-0.50003pt}\,\bigg)\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\sigma}\,,\\
\dot{p}_{\mu}\,&=\,qF_{\mu\nu}({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}})\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}}^{\nu}\,,\end{split}

(130)

where we have set
\kappa^{0}=1

and fixed
\kappa^{1}
and
\kappa^{2}

by demanding the preservation of
the four spin constraints.
When using Eq. (
129
),
the unique solution is
(assuming
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}\neq 0
)

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}^{1}\,=\,-\frac{i}{m^{2}}\,qF_{\rho\sigma}({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}})\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}}^{\rho}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\sigma}\,,\quad\bar{\kappa}^{2}\,=\,0\,.

(131)

It is easy to see that
Eq. (
130
) implies the
g{\>=\>}0
BMT equations.
Similarly,
one incorporates generic
g

by simply adding

(g/2)\hskip 1.00006ptq\hskip 1.00006pt{*}\hskip-0.50003ptF_{\mu\nu}({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}})\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}^{\mu}\hskip 0.24994pt\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}}^{\nu}

to the Lagrangian in Eq. (
129
).

The second approach is based on
an alternative coordinatization of the phase space
\mathds{P}_{16}^{\smash{\text{VO}}}

that arises by studying the Hamiltonian flows of
the constraints
p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}
and
p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}
:

\displaystyle\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{x^{\mu},\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\mu},\hskip 1.00006pt{\alpha}^{\mu},\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\psi}}}_{1}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,,

(132)

where
p{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}{\alpha}=0
and
\psi_{1}{\>\in\>}\mathbb{C}
.
Specifically, we have defined

\displaystyle{\alpha}^{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt:=\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{\delta}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{{\alpha}}}}^{\nu}\,,\quad{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\psi}}}_{1}\hskip 1.00006pt:=\hskip 1.00006pt-i\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{p}{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip-0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\alpha}}}}{\quad\implies\quad}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}\,=\,x^{\mu}-\frac{1}{|p|}\,\Big(\,{{\alpha}^{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\psi}}}_{1}+\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\psi}}}}_{1}}\,\Big)\,.

(133)

The free-theory symplectic potential then boils down to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\theta^{\circ}\,&=\,p_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}+\frac{i}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\bar{\alpha}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\alpha}^{\mu}-d\bar{\alpha}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt{\alpha}^{\mu}}\,\Big)+\frac{i}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\psi}}}}_{1}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\psi}}}_{1}-d{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\bar{\psi}}}}_{1}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\psi}}}_{1}}\,\Big)\,.\end{split}

(134)

As a result, the action in Eq. (
128
)
can be alternatively formulated
as a functional of
(x^{\mu},p_{\mu},{\alpha}^{\mu},{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\psi}}}_{1})
and
(\kappa^{0},\kappa^{1},\kappa^{2})
.
By integrating out the
(\kappa^{1},{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\psi}}}_{1})
sector, one obtains
6
6

6

When varying these types of actions, one should be careful about the fact that not all four complex components of
{\alpha}^{\mu}

are independent.
The same comment applies to
Eq. (
176
) as well.

\displaystyle\int\,\left[\,{\,\begin{aligned} &p_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\mu}+\frac{i}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\bar{\alpha}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{{\alpha}}^{\mu}-\dot{\bar{\alpha}}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt{\alpha}^{\mu}}\,\Big)+qA_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\mu}\\
&-\frac{\kappa^{0}}{2m}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{p^{2}+m^{2}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006ptd\tau-\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt{\alpha}^{2}-\frac{\bar{\kappa}^{2}}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\alpha}^{2}\end{aligned}\hskip 1.00006pt}\,\right]\,d\tau\,.

(135)

The
g{\>=\>}0
BMT equations can be derived from Eq. (
135
).
Incorporating generic
g
is easy.

Spherical Top

The spherical top
is a sigma model
\mathbb{R}{\>\to\>}\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}_{20}
,
whose target space
\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}_{20}
is coordinatized by
({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu},{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{A},p_{\mu},{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\mu\nu})
.
The reduced phase space
\mathds{P}^{\smash{\text{ST}}}_{14}

with no fake spinning degrees of freedom
arises by imposing the gauge generator constraints
\phi_{a}{\>=\>}0

that pair up with
the gauge-fixing condition
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\chi}}}^{a}{\>=\>}0
.
The imposition of
\phi_{a}{\>=\>}0
is a must,
while different choices exist for the gauge-fixing function
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\chi}}}^{a}
.

Eqs. (
111
) and (
52
)
together stipulates that the action
of the minimally coupled charged spherical top is

\displaystyle\int\,\left[\,{\,\begin{aligned} &p_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}}^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\,{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\mu\nu}\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{A}\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}}{}^{\nu}{}^{A}+qA_{\mu}\hskip-0.50003pt\hskip-0.24994pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}+{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}p/p^{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,\frac{d}{d\tau}\Big(\,{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu}+{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}^{\mu\nu}\hskip 0.50003ptp_{\nu}/p^{2}}\,\Big)\\
&-\frac{\kappa^{0}}{2m}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{p^{2}+m^{2}}\,\Big)-\kappa^{a}\hskip 1.00006pt\phi_{a}\end{aligned}\hskip 1.00006pt}\,\right]\,d\tau\,,

(136)

which is a functional of

({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}^{\mu},{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mu}{}_{A},p_{\mu},{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}_{\mu\nu})
and
(\kappa^{0},\kappa^{a})
.

Importantly, the action in Eq. (
136
)
allows for adopting one’s favorite SSC.
While the gauge generators
\phi_{a}
are explicitly incorporated in the action,
the gauge-fixing function
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\bm{\chi}}}^{a}
will be only specified

a posteriori
during the process of determining
\kappa^{a}
.
The action in Eq. (
136
)
itself is
agnostic of the choice of the SSC
and is spin gauge-invariant.
Hence, the situation differs from
the vector oscillator:
one cannot proactively replace

{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}+{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}p/p^{2}
in Eq. (
136
)
with
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}
.
Note that

{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}^{\mu\nu}p_{\nu}=0

iff one adopts the covariant gauge-fixing condition via Eq. (
55
).

A correct understanding on this point
facilitates
a consistent derivation of
the BMT equations
with
any
choice of the SSC.
For example,
the saddle of Eq. (
136
) is found
in the covariant spin gauge as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}}^{\mu}\,=\,\frac{p^{\mu}}{m}-\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}^{\mu\rho}}{m^{2}}\hskip 1.00006ptqF_{\rho\sigma}({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}})\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}}^{\sigma}&\,,\quad\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}}{}^{\mu}{}_{a}\,=\,-\bigg(\,{\frac{p^{\mu}\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}}^{\rho}}{m^{2}}\hskip 1.00006ptqF_{\rho\sigma}({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}})\hskip-0.50003pt}\,\bigg)\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\sigma}{}_{a}\,,\\
\dot{p}_{\mu}\,=\,qF_{\mu\nu}({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}})\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}}^{\nu}&\,,\quad\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}}^{\mu\nu}\,=\,-2\hskip 1.00006pt\bigg(\,{\frac{p^{[\mu|}\dot{{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}}}^{\rho}}{m^{2}}\hskip 1.00006ptqF_{\rho\sigma}({\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{x}})\hskip-0.50003pt}\,\bigg)\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}^{\sigma|\nu]}\,.\end{split}

(137)

Compare this with Eq. (
130
).
Eq. (
137
) readily derives the
g{\>=\>}0
BMT equations.
It is left as an exercise
to derive the BMT equations
in noncovariant spin gauges as well,
such as the canonical gauge in Eq. (
56
).
Incorporating generic
g
is straightforward.

6
Universality in Interacting Theory: Gravity

6.1
Couplings as Covariant Symplectic Perturbations

Let us now move on to gravitational interactions.
As a preliminary analysis,
we begin by describing
how the Souriau-Feynman method in Sec.
5.1

generalizes to gravity
csg
.

Flat Spacetime

We revisit the free scalar particle in Eq. (
100
).
The symplectic form
\omega^{\circ}
is described in
the coordinate one-form basis

(dx^{\mu},dp_{\mu})
,
where
\mu

is a
global Lorentz
index:

\displaystyle\omega^{\circ}\,=\,dp_{\mu}\wedge dx^{\mu}\,.

(138)

As an intermediate step toward general relativity,
it is always instructive to
investigate
“flat spacetime in noninertial frames.”
The particle’s position variable
x^{\mu}

becomes curvilinear coordinates
on the Minkowski space
\mathbb{M}=(\mathbb{R}^{4},\eta)
,
which behave as
x^{\mu}\mapsto x^{\prime\mu}{\>=\>}f^{\mu}(x)

under coordinate transformations.
The particle’s momentum variable is
p_{\mu}
,
which transforms as
p_{\mu}\mapsto p_{\nu}\hskip 1.00006pt(\partial x^{\nu}\hskip-1.00006pt/\partial x^{\prime\mu})
.

Crucially,
the coordinate basis
(dx^{\mu},dp_{\mu})
of one-forms
is not generally covariant,
since
dp_{\mu}
is not transformed to

dp_{\nu}\hskip 1.00006pt(\partial x^{\nu}\hskip-1.00006pt/\partial x^{\prime\mu})

due to a term

p_{\nu}\hskip 1.00006pt(\partial^{2}x^{\nu}\hskip-1.00006pt/\partial x^{\prime\mu}\partial x^{\prime\rho})\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\prime\rho}

that involves a second derivative.
As is well-known,
such second-derivative terms are typically canceled by
exploiting an affine connection.
In particular, let
\nabla
be the Levi-Civita connection of the flat metric
\eta
.
In curvilinear coordinates, it describes nonzero Christoffel symbols
\Gamma^{\mu}{}_{\nu\rho}(x)
.
Consider the covariant exterior derivative

Dp_{\mu}{\>=\>}dp_{\mu}{\>-\>}p_{\nu}\hskip 1.00006pt\Gamma^{\nu}{}_{\mu\rho}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\rho}
.
Then
(dx^{\mu},Dp_{\mu})
is a generally covariant basis of one-forms on
T^{*}\mathbb{M}
,
as

dx^{\mu}\mapsto(\partial x^{\prime\mu}\hskip-1.00006pt/\partial x^{\nu})\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\nu}

and

Dp_{\mu}\mapsto Dp_{\nu}\hskip 1.00006pt(\partial x^{\nu}\hskip-1.00006pt/\partial x^{\prime\mu})

under coordinate transformations.

In this covariant basis,
the free-theory symplectic form in
Eq. (
138
)
is represented as

\displaystyle\omega^{\circ}\,=\,Dp_{\mu}\wedge dx^{\mu}\,,

(139)

which is manifestly under coordinate transformations.

In fact,
a more sophisticated treatment
will employ the tetrad formalism.
Let
e^{m}{\>=\>}

e^{m}{}_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}

be the orthonormal coframe
such that
\eta_{\mu\nu}(x){\>=\>}\eta_{mn}\hskip 1.00006pte^{m}{}_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pte^{n}{}_{\nu}(x)
.
Here,
m,n,

r,s,\cdots\in\{0,1,2,3\}

are
local Lorentz
indices.
The components of the particle’s momentum
are
p_{m}
,
where
p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pte^{m}{}_{\mu}(x)=p_{\mu}
.
In this case,
the free-theory symplectic form is written as

\displaystyle\omega^{\circ}\,=\,Dp_{m}\wedge e^{m}\,,

(140)

which manifests invariances under
coordinate and local Lorentz transformations.
Here, the covariant exterior derivative
D

utilizes the spin connection.

The advantage of employing the tetrad formalism
is that the Hamiltonian in Eq. (
102
)
remains in the free-theory form:

\phi_{0}{\>=\>}(\eta^{mn}\hskip 0.50003ptp_{m}\hskip 0.24994ptp_{n}{\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt}m^{2})/2m
,
where
\eta^{mn}
describes a
constant
matrix.
In our view,
this refinement is necessary
for fully succeeding
the philosophy of the Souriau-Feynman method in Sec.
5.1
:
manifest covariance

and

fixed Hamiltonian.

Curved Spacetime

We are now ready to proceed to general relativity.
Spacetime is a smooth pseudo-Riemannian four-manifold
\mathcal{M}{\>=\>}(\mathbb{R}^{4},g)
.
Let
\nabla
be the Levi-Civita connection of the metric
g
,
whose connection coefficients are the Christoffel symbols

\Gamma^{\mu}{}_{\nu\rho}(x)
.
Let
e^{m}{\>=\>}e^{m}{}_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}
be an orthonormal coframe such that

g_{\mu\nu}(x)=

\eta_{mn}\hskip 1.00006pte^{m}{}_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pte^{n}{}_{\nu}(x)
.
Let
\gamma^{m}{}_{n}{\>=\>}\gamma^{m}{}_{n\rho}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\rho}
describe the spin connection.
The covariant exterior derivative is denoted as
D
.
As is well-known,
the Riemann curvature and torsion two-forms
are defined as

R^{m}{}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006ptR^{m}{}_{nrs}(x)\hskip 1.00006pte^{r}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}e^{s}=d\gamma^{m}{}_{n}+\gamma^{m}{}_{r}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}\gamma^{r}{}_{n}

and

T^{m}=De^{m}=de^{m}+\gamma^{m}{}_{n}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}e^{n}
,
the latter of which vanishes for the Levi-Civita connection.

The Hamiltonian formulation of
a scalar particle in
\mathcal{M}

takes the cotangent bundle
T^{*}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathcal{M}

as the phase space.
This phase space
will be endowed with
a nondegenerate two-form,

\displaystyle\omega^{\bullet}\,=\,Dp_{m}\wedge e^{m}\,.

(141)

Physically speaking, Eq. (
141
)
implements the free-theory symplectic form

dp_{\mu}\wedge dx^{\mu}

in local inertial frames.
This describes the covariantization map
csg

\displaystyle\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{dx^{\mu},\hskip 1.00006ptdp_{\mu}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,\,\mapsto\,\hskip 1.00006pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{e^{m},\hskip 1.00006ptDp_{m}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,,

(142)

which promotes
global Lorentz
indices to
local Lorentz
indices.

With this understanding,
the Einstein equivalence principle
implies that
the particle’s symplectic form

\omega

must take the form

\displaystyle\omega\,\,=\,\,\omega^{\bullet}\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt\omega^{\prime}\qquad\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{0.95}[0.95]{{\hbox{{\text{where}}}}}}\qquad\lim_{R\to 0}\omega^{\prime}\,=\,0\,.

(143)

This means that

\omega

approaches to

\omega^{\bullet}
in Eq. (
141
)
when curvature effects are ignored.
Said in another way,
physics in curved spacetime
should approximately look like
physics in flat spacetime
when examined in local Lorentz frames,
in the limit of negligible tidal effects.

Crucially,

\omega^{\prime}
in Eq. (
143
)
must be invariant under
coordinate and local Lorentz transformations,
as
\omega^{\bullet}
and
\omega
are.
Hence Eq. (
143
)
describes a covariant split of the gravity-coupled symplectic form
\omega
.
It understands
\omega

as a curvature-induced perturbation around
the covariantization
\omega^{\bullet}

of the free theory’s
\omega^{\circ}
.
In this sense,
we refer to
such
\omega^{\prime}

as a
covariant symplectic perturbation
.
7
7

7

See Ref.
csg
for its precise mathematical definition and formalization.

It should be clear that
this construction parallels Sec.
5.1
,
where the electromagnetism-coupled symplectic form
\omega

arises as a curvature-induced
(meaning the curvature
F
of the
\mathrm{U}(1)
gauge bundle)
perturbation around
the free theory’s
\omega^{\circ}
.

Again, an important consistency condition
is the Liouville property of classical time evolution,
which ensures the conservation of classical probability in a sense
Kim:2025sey
;
csg
.
The Liouville property is mathematically equivalent to the closure of the symplectic form,

d\omega=0
.
Imposing this closure on
\omega
in Eq. (
143
),
one finds that

\omega^{\prime}
must satisfy

\displaystyle d\omega^{\prime}\,=\,-d\omega^{\bullet}\,=\,-D^{2}p_{m}\wedge e^{m}+Dp_{m}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}De^{m}\,=\,p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{R^{m}{}_{n}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}e^{n}}\,\Big)\,.

(144)

Here, we have used the fact that
\omega^{\bullet}
carries no free indices, so

d\omega^{\bullet}=D\omega^{\bullet}
.
We have also used the definitions of the Riemann curvature and torsion two-forms.

The bracketed term in Eq. (
144
)
identically vanishes
by the algebraic Bianchi identity of the Riemann tensor.
Therefore, the simplest gravitational coupling
consistent with
gauge invariances,
equivalence principle,
and Liouville property
is achieved by

\omega^{\prime}=0
.
It turns out that

\omega^{\prime}=0
precisely realizes
the minimally coupled scalar particle
whose EoM are the geodesic equations,
as shown below.

Covariant Poisson Bracket Relation

Note that

(e^{m},Dp_{m})
provides a complete,
yet noncoordinate,
basis of one-forms on the phase space
T^{*}\mathcal{M}
.

The pointwise inverse of
\omega^{\bullet}

is the unique bivector
\Pi^{\bullet}

on
T^{*}\mathcal{M}

such that

\displaystyle{\Pi^{\bullet}\hskip-0.50003pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{e^{m}},{e^{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\hskip 1.00006pt=\hskip 1.00006pt0\,,\quad{\Pi^{\bullet}\hskip-0.50003pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{e^{m}},{Dp_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\hskip 1.00006pt=\hskip 1.00006pt\delta^{m}{}_{n}\,,\quad{\Pi^{\bullet}\hskip-0.50003pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{Dp_{m}},{Dp_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\hskip 1.00006pt=\hskip 1.00006pt0\,.

(145)

Eq. (
145
)
is nothing but the covariantization of
the free theory’s Poisson bracket relation in Eq. (
101
)
via the replacements

\Pi^{\circ}\mapsto\Pi^{\bullet}

and

(dx^{\mu},dp_{\mu})\mapsto(e^{m},Dp_{m})
:
recall Eq. (
142
).

Let
\Pi
be the pointwise inverse of the full symplectic form
\omega
.
Suppose the simplest covariant symplectic perturbation,

\omega^{\prime}=0
.
In this case, we have

\Pi=\Pi^{\bullet}
.
Notably,
Eq. (
145
)
facilitates a
manifestly covariant derivation

of the Hamiltonian EoM:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&e^{m}{}_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\mu}\,=\,{\Pi\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{e^{m}},{d\phi_{0}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,{\Pi\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{e^{m}},{D\phi_{0}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,{\Pi\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{e^{m}},{Dp_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{n}\,=\,\frac{p^{m}}{m}\,,\\
&\frac{Dp_{m}}{d\tau}\,=\,{\Pi\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{Dp_{m}},{d\phi_{0}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,{\Pi\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{Dp_{m}},{D\phi_{0}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,{\Pi\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{Dp_{m}},{Dp_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{n}\,=\,0\,.\end{split}

(146)

Namely, we insert the covariant one-forms
e^{m}
and
Dp_{m}

to the Poisson bivector
\Pi
.
We also use the fact that the Hamiltonian is a scalar function,
so
d\phi_{0}=D\phi_{0}
.
It is easy to check that Eq. (
146
)
reproduces the geodesic equation.

More explicitly,
let
E_{m}{\>=\>}E^{\mu}{}_{m}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{\mu}

be the frame vector fields (tetrad)
on the spacetime
that is dual to the coframe
e^{m}=e^{m}{}_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}
.
Namely,

e^{m}{}_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptE^{\mu}{}_{n}(x)=\delta^{m}{}_{n}

and

E^{\mu}{}_{m}(x)\hskip 1.00006pte^{m}{}_{\nu}(x)=\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}
.
It is not difficult to see that

(\tilde{E}_{m},\partial/\partial p_{m})

provides a covariant basis of vector fields
on the phase space,
where

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{r}\,=\,E^{\rho}{}_{r}(x)\,\bigg(\,{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\rho}}+p_{n}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma^{n}{}_{mr}(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{m}}}\,\bigg)\,.

(147)

In the mathematical jargon,

\tilde{E}_{r}
in
Eq. (
148
) is the
horizontal lift

ehresmann1948connexions
;
Mason:2013sva
;
gde
of the spacetime tetrad
E_{r}

to the phase space
with respect to the Levi-Civita connection.

It follows that

(\tilde{E}_{m},\partial/\partial p_{m})

is the dual basis to
(e^{m},Dp_{m})
.
As a result,
the bivector

\Pi^{\bullet}
in
Eq. (
145
)
is explicitly found as

\displaystyle\Pi^{\bullet}\,=\,\tilde{E}_{m}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{m}}\,.

(148)

It is left as an exercise to
examine the components of Eq. (
148
)
in any basis
to reproduce Eq. (
146
).

We end with
a useful formula.
Let
\omega=\omega^{\bullet}+\omega^{\prime}

in a generic phase space.
Let
\Pi^{\bullet}
and
\Pi
be
the pointwise inverses of
\omega^{\bullet}
and
\omega
.
Recalling Eq. (
109
),
it is easy to see that

\displaystyle\omega\,=\,\omega^{\bullet}\hskip 1.00006pt+\,\omega^{\prime}{\quad\implies\quad}\Pi\,=\,\Pi^{\bullet}-\Pi^{\bullet}\hskip 1.00006pt\omega^{\prime}\,\Pi\,.

(149)

In writing down Eq. (
149
), we have treated

\omega
,
\omega^{\bullet}
,
\omega^{\prime}
,
\Pi
,
\Pi^{\bullet}

like antisymmetric matrices.

6.2
Universal Covariant Poisson Bracket Relation

Next,
we formulate
a generalization of
the universal Poisson bracket relation in Eq. (
99
)
to describe spinning particles
with curvilinear coordinates.

Suppose a free massive spinning particle in Minkowski space
\mathbb{M}
,
formulated on a symplectic manifold
\mathds{P}
as a phase space.
As reviewed in Sec.
2.2
,
Poincaré symmetry implies
the existence of functions
(x^{\mu},\hat{y}^{\mu},p_{\mu})
on
\mathds{P}

that satisfy
the universal Poisson bracket relation in Eq. (
99
)

sst-asym
.
Mathematically, Eq. (
99
)
plugs in the one-forms

(dx^{\mu},d\hat{y}^{\mu},dp_{\mu})

to the free-theory Poisson bivector
\Pi^{\circ}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\Pi^{\circ}\hskip-0.50003pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{dx^{\mu}},{dx^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,&=\,\frac{1}{-p^{2}}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\hat{y}_{\rho}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\sigma}\,=\,{\Pi^{\circ}\hskip-0.50003pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{d\hat{y}^{\mu}},{d\hat{y}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,,\\
{\Pi^{\circ}\hskip-0.50003pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{dx^{\mu}},{d\hat{y}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,&=\,\frac{1}{-p^{2}}\,\Big(\,{\hat{y}^{\mu}p^{\nu}+p^{\mu}\hat{y}^{\nu}}\,\Big)\,,\\[1.79993pt]
{\Pi^{\circ}\hskip-0.50003pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{dx^{\mu}},{dp_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,&=\,\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\,,\\[2.57996pt]
{\Pi^{\circ}\hskip-0.50003pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{d\hat{y}^{\mu}},{dp_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,&=\,0\,,\\[1.79993pt]
{\Pi^{\circ}\hskip-0.50003pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{dp_{\mu}},{dp_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,&=\,0\,.\end{split}

(150)

Now suppose the same particle
is put in a curved spacetime
\mathcal{M}=(\mathbb{R}^{4},g)
,
in which case
the phase space will be a symplectic manifold
\mathcal{P}

such that

\dim\mathcal{P}=\dim\mathds{P}
.
Let
