(333)

On the other hand,
the rotational sector of the gravity-coupled symplectic potential
describes

\frac{1}{2}\>S_{mn}\hskip 1.00006pt\Lambda^{mA}\hskip 1.00006ptD\Lambda^{n}{}_{A}\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\>S_{mn}\hskip 1.00006pt\Lambda^{mA}\hskip 1.00006ptd\Lambda^{n}{}_{A}-\frac{1}{2}\>S_{mn}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma^{mn}{}_{\rho}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\rho}
,
which identifies the “spin part” of the interaction action as

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\smash{\text{spin}}}\,=\,\int dt\,\,\bigg(\,{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.0391,0.0391}{}{-S_{0i}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma^{0i}{}_{0}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.4297,0.957}{}-\frac{1}{2}\>S_{ij}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma^{ij}{}_{k}\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{k}}-\frac{1}{2}\>S_{ij}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma^{ij}{}_{0}}\,\bigg)\,.

(334)

By inserting Eq. (
331b
)
and recalling some basic facts concerning the canonical gauge in Eq. (
323
),
we arrive at the desired spin vertex factors:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{spin}}\,=\,\int dt\,\,\bigg(\,{\bigg(\,{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.0391,0.0391}{}\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt{\color[rgb]{0.0391,0.4297,0.957}{}1}}\,\bigg)\,\varepsilon^{i}{}_{jk}\,\partial_{i}\phi\,S^{j}\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{k}\,+\,\frac{1}{4}\>\varepsilon^{kij}\hskip 0.24994ptS_{k}\hskip 0.50003ptF_{ij}}\,\bigg)\,.

(335)

Note that the factors
{\scalebox{1.025}[1]{$\mathrm{e}$}}^{\phi}
and
{\scalebox{1.025}[1]{$\mathrm{e}$}}^{3\phi}
in Eq. (
331b
)
can be safely ignored
when deriving this expression.

Leading Spin-Orbit Coupling

The propagators of PN gravity are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{\phi(\vec{x}_{1})\hskip 1.00006pt\phi(\vec{x}_{2})}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,=\,-\frac{G}{r}\,,\quad\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{A_{i}(\vec{x}_{1})\hskip 1.00006ptA_{j}(\vec{x}_{2})}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,=\,\frac{4G}{r}\,\delta_{ij}\,.\end{split}

(336)

From the above propagators
and
the leading-order interaction actions in
Eqs. (
333
) and (
335
),
we obtain the PN effective Hamiltonian
encoding
the leading-order spin-orbit coupling:

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{SO}}^{\mathrm{LO}}\,=\,-\frac{Gm_{2}}{r^{2}}\>\vec{S}_{1}\cdot\bigg(\,{\frac{3}{2}\,\vec{v}_{1}{\hskip 1.00006pt\times\hskip 1.00006pt}\hat{n}-2\hskip 1.00006pt\vec{v}_{2}{\hskip 1.00006pt\times\hskip 1.00006pt}\hat{n}}\,\bigg)+(1{\hskip 1.00006pt\leftrightarrow\hskip 1.00006pt}2)\,.

(337)

Compared to the original derivation in Ref.
Levi:2015msa
, our computation is more streamlined in a few ways.
We need not deal with any acceleration term
(a^{i}{\>=\>}\ddot{x}^{i})
or a redefinition of the position variable
x^{i}
.
We stay in the canonical gauge throughout; there is no need for switching between the two gauge choices during the derivation.
The absence of an acceleration term in the leading spin vertex factors, Eq. (
335
), keeps affecting higher order terms in the PN expansion.
For example, the
\vec{a}_{1}
term in Eq. (6.14) of Ref.
Levi:2015msa
does not appear in our framework.

Fictitious Vertices in Feynman Rules

Yet another implication of our work on the PN expansion is that
the
fictitious
Feynman vertices involving the
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\bm{b}\hskip 0.12497pt}
vector are unavoidable,
as is clearly stated in Eq. (
219
).
The fictitious terms
are present in the canonical spin gauge.
The only spin gauge in which the fictitious terms are absent
is the covariant spin gauge.

For a simple example where the fictitious vertices cannot be avoided, consider the computation of the NNLO spin-squared potential in Ref.
Levi:2015ixa
. When extracting the spin-squared vertices from the worldline action, we should include the
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\bm{b}\hskip 0.12497pt}
term and the
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\bm{b}\hskip 0.12497pt}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\bm{b}\hskip 0.12497pt}
term in Eq. (
266
), as well as the quadrupolar coupling with the constant coefficient
C_{2}
in Eq. (
161
), with the
1{\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt}3
decomposition specified in Eq. (
323
) and Eq. (
325
).
If we proceed to the spin-cubic potential, we should include the
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\bm{b}\hskip 0.12497pt}{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\bm{b}\hskip 0.12497pt}
term and the
{\color[rgb]{0.8789,0.1602,0.5313}\bm{b}\hskip 0.12497pt}^{3}
term in Eq. (
266
), the fictitious coupling descending from the quadrupolar coupling in Eq. (
161
),
and the genuine cubic coupling we are not discussing in this paper.
The pattern will continue with a proliferation of fictitious vertices at higher orders.

9.4
Higher Multipoles and Beyond

Linear Couplings

As is glimpsed in Eqs. (
117
) and (
161
),
our universal frameworks in Secs.
5
and
6

readily generalize to all multipole orders.
For electromagnetism,
an ansatz that conforms to the physical principles in Eq. (
118
) is

\displaystyle\omega\,=\,\omega^{\circ}\,+\,\frac{1}{2}\,qF_{\mu\nu}(x)\,dx^{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{\nu}\,+\,q\,d\hskip 1.00006pt\bigg[\,{\,\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{c_{\ell}}{\ell!}\,\hskip 1.00006pt{*^{\ell}}\hskip-0.50003ptF_{\rho_{1}\sigma,\rho_{2}\cdots\rho_{\ell}}(x)\,\hat{y}^{\rho_{1}}\hat{y}^{\rho_{2}}\cdots\hat{y}^{\rho_{\ell}}\,dx^{\sigma}\hskip 1.00006pt}\,\bigg]\vphantom{\bigg|}\,,

(338)

where
*^{\ell}
means to apply the Hodge star
\ell
times.
Eq. (
338
) achieves
the most general linear electromagnetic coupling,
where
the coefficient
c_{\ell}
parameterizes
the spin-induced
2^{\ell}
-pole moment.
Similarly,
an ansatz that conforms to the physical principles in Eq. (
162
) is

\displaystyle\omega\,\,=\,\,\omega^{\bullet}\,+\,p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt{\star}R^{m}{}_{n}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}^{n}\,+\,d\hskip 1.00006pt\bigg[\,{\,\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{C_{\ell}}{\ell!}\,\hskip 1.00006ptp_{m}\,{\star^{\ell}}\hskip-0.50003ptR^{m}{}_{r_{1}r_{2}s;r_{3};\cdots;r_{\ell}}\hskip-0.50003pt(x)\,\hat{y}^{r_{1}}\hat{y}^{r_{2}}\hat{y}^{r_{3}}\hskip-1.00006pt\cdots\hat{y}^{r_{\ell}}\,e^{s}\hskip 1.00006pt}\,\bigg]\vphantom{\bigg|}\,.

(339)

Eq. (
339
) is the transcription of the Levi-Steinhoff action
Levi:2015msa

in the symplectic perturbation framework,
which achieves
the most general linear-in-Riemann gravitational coupling.
The Wilson coefficient
C_{\ell}

parameterizes the spin-induced mass
2^{\ell}
-pole moment.

Note that the Hodge star implements parity invariance.
Thus

c_{2k}
and
C_{2k}
are electric moments,
while

c_{2k+1}
and
C_{2k+1}
are magnetic moments.

Nonlinear Couplings

The above multipole coefficients
c_{\ell}
and
C_{\ell}

dictate only the linear couplings
to external fields.
At the nonlinear level,
there are broad possibilities for generalization.
We may want to illuminate two categories of terms,
in particular:

(a)

Ordinary terms
(
dx
,
e
):

\displaystyle\smash{\kern-3.00003pt\frac{q^{2}}{m^{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt(\hskip 0.50003pt{\hat{y}^{\mu}F_{\mu\rho}(x)\hskip 0.50003ptF^{\rho}{}_{\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt)\hskip 1.00006pt(\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{\kappa}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\kappa}}\hskip 0.24994pt)\,,\,\,\,p\hskip 1.00006ptR(x)\hskip 0.12497ptR(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}\hat{y}\hat{y}\hat{y}\hskip 1.00006pte\,.\kern-3.00003pt}

(340a)

(b)

Spin-derivative terms
(
d\hat{y}
,
D\hat{y}
):

\displaystyle\smash{qF_{\mu\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}^{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptd\hat{y}^{\nu}\,,\quad p_{m}\hskip 0.50003pt{*}R^{m}{}_{nrs}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}^{n}\hat{y}^{r}D\hat{y}^{s}\,,\quad p\,{*}R(x)\hskip 0.50003ptR(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}\hat{y}\hat{y}\hat{y}\hskip 1.00006ptD\hat{y}\,.}

(340b)

Here,
we have enumerated
perturbations on the symplectic potential.

The systematic enumeration of all possible terms
at a given order
is an important problem,
although it goes beyond the present paper’s scope.
Generally speaking, this counting will depend on
the field basis choice
(symplectic versus Hamiltonian perturbations),
while computing scattering amplitudes can provide some guidance.
As a reminder, our symplectic perturbation approach
specifies a particular worldline field basis
by fixing the mass-shell constraint in the free-theory form
\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt(\eta^{mn}p_{m}p_{n}+m^{2})
;
see App.
C.3
for a related discussion.

The above two classes of terms
play different roles in worldline perturbation theory.
Consider the diagrammatic computation of two-quanta Compton amplitudes,
for instance.
The example terms listed in Eq. (
340a
)
contribute
to the amplitude
as
contact vertices
.
In contrast,
the example terms in Eq. (
340b
)
contribute essentially
via
exchange channels

due to propagation of worldline fluctuations,
as the background worldline
will exhibit
\dot{\hat{y}}=0
.

This point should also justify
the identification of the spin-derivative terms in Eq. (
340b
) as “nonlinear couplings.”
Although
some of them are linear in the curvature tensors,
they are still nonlinear in the sense that
they do not contribute to the three-point amplitudes with one massless quantum.
Their effects on the classical EoM,
however,
could be just as significant as
the ordinary terms
at the same curvature order.

As shown by Refs.
probe-nj
;
njmagic.1
;
njmagic.11
,
spin-derivative terms are
necessary

for achieving black hole couplings
(see Eq. (
353
)).
Also, they may not be so fundamentally different than the ordinary terms
in the spinspacetime perspective of Sec.
9.5
.

Definition of Black Hole Coupling

Physically, the nonlinear couplings will encode various tidal deformability effects, taking different values for each astrophysical object.

A particularly intriguing case is black holes,
which have attracted considerable attention in the current literature.
The determination of the exact black hole coupling to all orders
has been a long-standing problem.
The central question reads

.
“
What defines black holes

among all massive

spinning objects
in the point-particle effective theory?
”

The problem stands unambiguously solved
at least
in the following subsectors.

(a)

In
Linearized Gravity
:
The Kerr black hole is defined by
the
unity of multipole coefficients
,

C_{\ell}=1
for all
\ell=2,3,4,\cdots

Hansen:1974zz
;
Newman:1965tw-janis
;
Hernandez:1967zza
;
Thorne:1980ru

(cf. Refs.
ahh2017
;
Guevara:2018wpp
;
Guevara:2019fsj
;
chkl2019
;
aho2020
).

(b)

In
Self-Dual Gravity
:
The Kerr black hole is defined by
a
superintegrability

in type-D backgrounds
with Killing-Yano tensors

probe-nj

(cf. Refs.
Johansson:2019dnu
;
Aoude:2020onz
;
Lazopoulos:2021mna
).

We have reviewed this development in App.
D

as a preliminary for Sec.
9.5
.
If a worldline model passes the above tests (a) and (b),
then it qualifies as an effective description of the Kerr black hole
according to our best theoretical understanding
at the current moment.

Criterion (a) is necessary for reproducing the Newman-Janis
Newman:1965tw-janis
property
in linearized gravity.
The Newman-Janis shift
describes that
the Kerr black hole is
in some sense
a Schwarzschild black hole
displaced into “complex spacetime”
along an imaginary direction
set by its spin length pseudovector.
Criterion (b) arises via a nonlinear generalization of this property
in curved backgrounds
probe-nj
.

The problem of constructing worldline models for black holes
may have been approached in model-specific manners
so far:
spherical top model
gmoov
,
massive twistor model
njmagic.1
;
njmagic.11
,
fermionic model
bonocore2025higher
,
etc.
Meanwhile, Ref.
probe-nj
has explicated
the model-independent nature of the problem
by introducing a probe counterpart of the Newman-Janis
Newman:1965tw-janis
algorithm
in terms of the universal variables
(x,\hat{y},p)

and providing
the definition of black holes in terms of global symmetries
(criterion (b) above).

In this paper, we hope to
illuminate this model-independent aspect
again.
For a quick demonstration,
we will concern the electromagnetic analog of the Kerr black hole:
The zero-gravitation limit

G_{\mathrm{N}}{\>\to\>}0

of the Kerr-Newman solution,
dubbed the
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
solution

aho2020
;
Lynden-Bell:2002dvr
.
The
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
solution
exhibits
the unity of multipole coefficients

Newman:1965tw-janis
;
Newman:1973yu
;
Lynden-Bell:2002dvr
:

c_{\ell}{\>=\>}1
for all
\ell{\>=\>}1,2,3,\cdots
.
Its dynamics as a probe
enjoys an analogous superintegrability.

9.5
Black Holes

Hidden Complex Geometry of Universal Phase Space

Recall Sec.
2.2
,
where we reviewed the fact
sst-asym
that
the universal Poisson bracket relation in Eq. (
5
)
holds as a direct implication of
Poincarè symmetry.

In fact,
we can now disclose to the reader that
the universal Poisson bracket relation
admits a remarkably simpler presentation
sst-asym
.
Consider the complex combination,

\displaystyle z^{\mu}\,=\,x^{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 0.50003pti\hat{y}^{\mu}\,.

(341)

Straightforward algebra shows that Eq. (
5
)
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{z^{\mu}},{z^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,\,

\displaystyle=\,\,0\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{z^{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{\mu}},{p_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,0\,,

(342a)

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{z^{\mu}},{\bar{z}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,\,

\displaystyle=\,\,\frac{1}{p^{2}}\,\Big(\,{\delta^{\mu}{}_{\rho}\hskip 1.00006pt\delta^{\nu}{}_{\sigma}+\delta^{\mu}{}_{\sigma}\hskip 1.00006pt\delta^{\nu}{}_{\rho}+i\hskip 1.00006pt\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}{}_{\rho\sigma}\hskip-1.00006pt}\,\Big)\,(z^{\rho}{\hskip 1.00006pt-\,}\bar{z}^{\rho})\,p^{\sigma}\,,

(342b)

which fits on two lines.
Many components are set to zero.
This rewriting
not only provides a succinct summary
of Eq. (
5
)
but also reveals that
the the components of
z^{\mu}
in Eq. (
341
)
are Poisson-commutative,
despite the peculiar noncommutativity of
the physical center
coordinates
x^{\mu}

in Eq. (
5
).

Following
sst-asym
,
we refer to
z^{\mu}
as
spinspacetime coordinates
,
as they unify spacetime
x^{\mu}
and spin
\hat{y}^{\mu}

as real and imaginary parts.
Mathematically,
they are the holomorphic coordinates
for the complexified Minkowski space
\mathbb{M}^{\mathbb{C}}=(\mathbb{C}^{4},\eta^{\mathbb{C}})

whose real section is the flat spacetime
\mathbb{M}=(\mathbb{R}^{4},\eta)
.
The property that

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{z^{\mu}},{z^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,0\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{z^{\mu}},{\bar{z}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,\neq\,0\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\bar{z}^{\mu}},{\bar{z}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,0

(343)

is referred to as the zig-zag structure,
as the nicknames “zig” and “zag”
are used for referring to
“holomorphic” (
z
) and “anti-holomorphic” (
\bar{z}
),
respectively

sst-asym
.
14
14

14

This terminology is inspired by Penrose

penr04-zigzag
.

It should be evident that
spinspacetime and its zig-zag structure exist
universally
in
every special-relativistic massive spinning system.
In fact,
glimpses toward spinspacetime
had occurred in Eqs. (
40
), (
68
), and (
70
):
the models with less redundancies.

Inherent Association Between Holomorphy and Self-Duality

The idea of spinspacetime
traces back to
Newman and Winicour
newman1974curiosity
.
15
15

15

See also
Refs.
newman1974collection
;
newman1988remarkable
;
Newman:1973afx
;
Newman:2004ba
;
Newman:1973yu
;
Newman:2002mk
;
ko1981theory
;
grg207flaherty
,
which portray
Newman’s serious take on spinspacetime.

The observation was that
the self-dual part of the total angular momentum in Eq. (
7
) is

\displaystyle J\,=\,(x\wedge p)+{*}(\hat{y}\wedge p){\quad\implies\quad}J^{+}\,=\,(z\wedge p)^{+}\,.

(344)

Here, the
+
superscript
signifies self-dual projection by

\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt(1{\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip 1.00006pti\hskip 1.00006pt{*})
.
The Hodge duality
{*}
in Eq. (
344
) turns into the imaginary unit
+i
in the self-dual sector,
so holomorphic coordinates
z^{\mu}=x^{\mu}+i\hat{y}^{\mu}

arise from the self-dual angular momentum
J^{+}
.
In the same way, anti-holomorphic coordinates
\bar{z}^{\mu}=x^{\mu}-i\hat{y}^{\mu}

arise from the anti-self-dual angular momentum
J^{-}
.
Importantly, an inherent association arises between

holomorphy and self-duality

in spinspacetime,
consistently with the fact that
\hat{y}^{\mu}
is a
pseudo
vector.

Newman and Winicour
newman1974curiosity
’s discussion on spinspacetime,
however,
does not examine
its Poisson structure
in Eq. (
342
).
The modern reboot of the spinspacetime program
sst-asym

proposes that one should pay attention to the Poisson structure of spinspacetime
and its zig-zag property,
as they are physical, universal structures.

For instance,
it is instructive to employ the spinor notation,
in which case Eq. (
342
) is boiled down to a further succinct form:

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{z^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}},{p_{\smash{{\beta}{\dot{{\beta}}}}\vphantom{{\beta}}}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,\delta^{\dot{{\alpha}}}{}_{\smash{{\dot{{\beta}}}}\vphantom{{\beta}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\delta_{\smash{{\beta}}\vphantom{{\beta}}}{}^{\alpha}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{z^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}},{\bar{z}^{{\dot{{\beta}}}{\beta}}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,\frac{1}{-p^{2}}\,(z{\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 1.00006pt}\bar{z})^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\beta}}\hskip 0.50003ptp^{{\dot{{\beta}}}{\alpha}}\,.

(345)

Eq. (
345
) is a one-line summary of the entirety of universal Poisson brackets in Eq. (
5
).

The chiral nature of the zig-zag bracket

\{\hskip 0.50003pt{z^{{\dot{{\alpha}}}{\alpha}}},{\bar{z}^{{\dot{{\beta}}}{\beta}}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}
in Eq. (
345
)
reflects the aforementioned association between
holomorphy and self-duality,
which
dictates the precession behavior of spinning particles under influence of external fields
and derives the probe-level Newman-Janis shift
from an ideal spin precession behavior

ambikerr1
;
sst-asym
.

Figure 6:

Spinspacetime offers a new angle on spin precession.
The Dirac gyromagnetic ratio
g{\>=\>}2

can be geometrically characterized
as the synchronization of
orbital and spin precessions in external fields.
When seen from spinspacetime,
such a motion appears as an isotropic spiral trajectory,
pointing to the equal blending of all
dx,d\hat{y}
components in
dz{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dz
.
In contrast, the traditional minimal coupling
g{\>=\>}0

translates to a flattened sinusoidal curve,
which points to
the reality of
dx{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx
.
Spinspacetime identifies
g{\>=\>}2

as the simplest coupling.

The Simplest Symplectic Perturbation from Spinspacetime

Spinspacetime opens a new chapter in relativity, offering a unique perspective on spinning-particle mechanics in four dimensions. It is now not only
space
and
time
but also
spin
that are unified.
This invites a different way of thinking:
spin is the imaginary part of spacetime.
Spacetime and spin may not be fundamentally distinct.
Fig.
6
invites this shift.

An electromagnetic field on spacetime
takes the form
\frac{1}{2}\,F_{\mu\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{\nu}
.
Spacetime
\mathbb{M}
is embedded in spinspacetime
\mathbb{M}^{\mathbb{C}}
as the real section.
Spacetime fields would somehow permeate into spinspacetime.
This will describe

dz{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dz
,

dz{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}d\bar{z}
,
or

d\bar{z}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}d\bar{z}

components.

For
charged scalar particles,
electromagnetic fields
are symplectic perturbations
(Sec.
5.1
).
For
charged spinning particles,

spinspacetime fields

will be
symplectic perturbations.

For scalar particles,

\omega^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}\,qF_{\mu\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{\nu}

is said to be the minimal coupling
because it is the simplest symplectic perturbation
that one can possibly write down.
For spinning particles,
the simplest symplectic perturbation on
spinspacetime

will declare the true minimal coupling.

The spacetime field
splits into self-dual and anti-self-dual parts as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,F_{\mu\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{\nu}\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\,F^{+}_{\mu\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\,F^{-}_{\mu\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{\nu}\,.

(346)

When studying how spacetime fields permeate into spinspacetime,
it can be helpful to examine their self-dual and anti-self-dual parts separately.

Recalling the earlier association,
suppose the self-dual field
is
holomorphic

on spinspacetime:
16
16

16

Note that the argument of
F^{+}_{\mu\nu}(z)
here had to be also holomorphic,
due to the principle of symplecticity (closure
d\omega^{\prime}=0
).

\displaystyle\omega^{\prime}\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\,qF^{+}_{\mu\nu}(z)\hskip 1.00006ptdz^{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dz^{\nu}\,.

(347)

Holomorphic symplectic perturbations are
the simplest symplectic perturbations
on spinspacetime,
in the sense that it minimally deforms the universal Poisson brackets in Eq. (
343
).
Holomorphic symplectic perturbations
preserve the Poisson-commutativity of holomorphic coordinates,
due to the very zig-zag structure in Eq. (
343
)
(recall Eq. (
109
)):

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{z^{\mu}},{z^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,\,=\,\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{z^{\mu}},{z^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt{z^{\mu}},{z^{\rho}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,\hskip 0.50003ptqF^{+}_{\rho\sigma}(z)\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{z^{\sigma}},{z^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,\,=\,\,0\,.

(348)

In this manner, only the zag-zag bracket
is deformed by the zig symplectic perturbation:

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{z^{\mu}},{z^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{z^{\mu}},{\bar{z}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,\neq\,0\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\bar{z}^{\mu}},{\bar{z}^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,\neq\,0\,.

(349)

We should understand the physics due to
the simplest symplectic perturbation in Eq. (
347
).
By straightforward application of
the methodology in Sec.
5.2
,
the EoM are derived from the universal Poisson brackets in Eq. (
342
)—equivalently Eq. (
345
), if one likes to work with spinors—that

\displaystyle\dot{z}^{\mu}\,=\,\hat{p}^{\mu}\,,\quad\dot{\bar{z}}^{\mu}\,=\,\hat{p}^{\mu}-\frac{2iq}{m}\,F^{+}{}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(z)\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}^{\nu}\,,\quad\dot{p}^{\mu}\,=\,\frac{q}{m}\,F^{+}{}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(z)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\nu}\,.

(350)

We see that
the EoM for the zig coordinates are
completely ignorant of the external field,
due to the very Poisson-commutativity shown in Eq. (
348
):

\dot{z}^{\mu}=\hat{p}^{\mu}
.

Eq. (
350
) translates to

\displaystyle\dot{z}^{\mu}\,=\,\hat{p}^{\mu}\,,\quad\dot{\hat{y}}^{\mu}\,=\,\frac{q}{m}\,F^{+}{}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(z)\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}^{\nu}\,,\quad\dot{p}^{\mu}\,=\,\frac{q}{m}\,F^{+}{}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(z)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\nu}\,,

(351)

which describes that
the orbital (
\hat{p}^{\mu}
) and spin (
\hat{y}^{\mu}
) precessions are perfectly synchronized
by the cyclotron angular frequency

(q/m)\hskip 1.00006ptF^{+}{}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(z)
.
Recalling the preliminary exploration in Fig.
6
,
this synchronization implies the gyromagnetic ratio
g{\>=\>}2
, which is
c_{1}{\>=\>}1
.
17
17

17

If self-dual fields were anti-holomorphic in spinspacetime,
then the particle exhibits
g=-2
,
which deviates from the black hole behavior.
See also Ref.
sst-asym
for
a physical interpretation of
the synchronization of orbital and spin precessions
for gravitational interactions
(“spinning equivalence principle”).

In fact,
we will see shortly that
all multipole coefficients are unity:

c_{\ell}{\>=\>}1
for all
\ell{\>=\>}1,2,3,\cdots
.

The Simplest Real Symplectic Perturbation from Spinspacetime

The symplectic perturbation in Eq. (
347
)
is formal,
giving rise to a complexified Poisson structure as in Eq. (
349
).
For a real
dynamics,
we add the missing complex conjugate
so that the anti-self-dual field is restored:

\displaystyle\omega^{\prime}\,

\displaystyle=\,\frac{1}{2}\>qF^{+}_{\mu\nu}(z)\hskip 1.00006ptdz^{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dz^{\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\>qF^{-}_{\mu\nu}(\bar{z})\hskip 1.00006ptd\bar{z}^{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}d\bar{z}^{\nu}\,.

(352)

By methods in differential geometry
gde
,
it follows that Eq. (
352
)
describes

njmagic.1
;
njmagic.11
18
18

18

To elaborate,
the potential one-form for
Eq. (
352
) can be represented as

\theta^{\prime}=\cos(\pounds_{N})\hskip 1.00006pt(A_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu})+\sinc(\pounds_{N})\hskip 1.00006pt({*}F_{\mu\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}^{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\nu})

via
F^{\pm}=(1\mp i\hskip 1.00006pt{*})F/2
,
where
N
is a vector field such that

\iota_{N}dx^{\mu}=\hat{y}^{\mu}
,

\iota_{N}d\hat{y}^{\mu}=0
,
and

\iota_{N}dp_{\mu}=0
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\omega^{\prime}\,=\,{}&{}\frac{1}{2}\,qF_{\mu\nu}(x)\,dx^{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{\nu}\,+\,q\,d\hskip 1.00006pt\bigg[\,{\,\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{1}{\ell!}\,\hskip 1.00006pt{*^{\ell}}\hskip-0.50003ptF_{\rho_{1}\sigma,\rho_{2}\cdots\rho_{\ell}}(x)\,\hat{y}^{\rho_{1}}\hat{y}^{\rho_{2}}\cdots\hat{y}^{\rho_{\ell}}\,dx^{\sigma}\hskip 1.00006pt}\,\bigg]\,,\\[-1.20007pt]
{}&{}\,+\,q\,d\hskip 1.00006pt\bigg[\,{\,\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{(\ell{\,-\hskip 1.00006pt}1)}{\ell!}\,\hskip 1.00006pt{*^{\ell}}\hskip-0.50003ptF_{\rho_{1}\sigma,\rho_{2}\cdots\rho_{\ell-1}}(x)\,\hat{y}^{\rho_{1}}\hat{y}^{\rho_{2}}\cdots\hat{y}^{\rho_{\ell-1}}\,d\hat{y}^{\sigma}\hskip 1.00006pt}\,\bigg]\,.\end{split}

(353)

Compare Eq. (
353
) with Eq. (
338
).
For the linear couplings,
we identify the unity
c_{\ell}{\>=\>}1
of multipole moments.
Thus Eq. (
353
) passes the first test of spinning black holes,
criterion (a).
Furthermore,
Eq. (
353
) also describes
nonlinear couplings
via

spin-derivative terms
,
which are necessary for passing the second test of spinning black holes.
19
19

19

For instance,
it can be diagrammatically shown
njmagic.11

that the resulting same-helicity Compton amplitudes
exhibit spin exponentiation for all multiplicities.
Thus Eq. (
352
)
is the symplectic perturbation
of the
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
spinning particle
up to mixed-chirality terms
(earthly deformations
probe-nj
)
that vanish in the self-dual limit,
such as
(q^{2}/m^{2})\hskip 1.00006pt(\hat{y}\hskip 1.00006ptF^{+}(x)\hskip 0.50003ptF^{-}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y})\hskip 1.00006pt(p\hskip 1.00006ptdx)
.

The message here is that the simplest symplectic perturbation from spinspacetime
directly yields
the simplest (black hole) coupling.
This fact is obscured in typical spacetime-based approaches;
it is a unique insight that spinspacetime can provide.
Also, it should be clear that
this derivation of a black hole coupling
is model independent.

The traditional minimal coupling,
c_{\ell}{\>=\>}0
,
arises by
the simplest symplectic perturbation from the standards of spacetime:

\frac{1}{2}\,qF_{\mu\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{\nu}
.
The spinspacetime Poisson brackets in Eq. (
342
) shows that
this grossly complicates the dynamics of the particle,
as
dx{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx

contains all the

dz{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dz
,

dz{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}d\bar{z}
,
and

d\bar{z}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}d\bar{z}

components.

Model-Independent Derivation of
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
EoM

Via Eqs. (
109
) and (
342
),
it is easy to
derive the complete
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
EoM
due to the real symplectic perturbation in Eq. (
352
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{p}_{\mu}\,&=\,qF^{+}_{\mu\nu}(z)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{z}^{\mu}+qF^{-}_{\mu\nu}(\bar{z})\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{\bar{z}}^{\mu}\,,\\
\dot{z}^{\mu}\,&=\,\hat{p}^{\mu}-\frac{2iq}{m^{2}}\,\Big(\,{\hat{y}^{\mu}p^{\nu}+p^{\mu}\hat{y}^{\nu}-i\hskip 1.00006pt\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\hat{y}_{\rho}p_{\sigma}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006ptF^{-}_{\nu\kappa}(\bar{z})\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{\bar{z}}^{\kappa}\,.\end{split}

(354)

The EoM for
\bar{z}^{\mu}
follow by complex conjugation
since the dynamics is real.
The above equations have been known from the massive twistor model
Kim:2024grz
.
Here, we have reproduced them in the model-agnostic fashion.
Note that Eq. (
354
) describes
all-orders-in-spin
EoM.

It is left as an exercise to
deduce the EoM for
x^{\mu}
and
y^{\mu}

by taking real and imaginary parts.
One may use an identity

\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\phi^{\pm}_{\nu\kappa}\hskip 1.00006ptu^{\kappa}=\mp\hskip 1.00006pt3\hskip 0.24994pti\hskip 1.00006pt\phi^{\pm}{}^{[\rho\sigma}u^{\mu]}

to remove the epsilon tensors
if wanted,
where
\phi^{+}_{\mu\nu}
(
\phi^{-}_{\mu\nu}
) is self-dual (anti-self-dual).

Overall, spinspacetime is a powerful tool
for making predictions on the all-orders-in-spin dynamics of spinning objects.

As demonstrated in Ref.
ambikerr1
,
spinspacetime is also useful for
deriving the all-orders-in-spin EoM of particles with generic multipole moments.
In this case,
one perturbs away from the black hole coupling by
redefining the Wilson coefficients on the complex worldline.

Dynamical Newman-Janis Shift of Conserved Charges

As a glimpse toward the applications of the above EoM,
we provide a reproduction of
the fact
njmagic.11
that
an idealized version of the spinning black hole binary problem
exhibits superintegrability
via exact hidden symmetries.
We follow the model-independent presentation due to Ref.
probe-nj
.
This discussion confirms the criterion (b),
confirming that Eq. (
353
) qualifies as an effective point-particle description of
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
.

The dream of a theoretical relativist
is to understand the exact dynamics of the
Kerr-Kerr black hole binary system.
However, the problem is too hard.
As a first simplification,
we suppose that one of the black holes is significantly heavier than the other:
the zeroth self-force limit.
As a second simplification,
we suppose the electromagnetic analog:
the
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
-
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
binary problem.

As a result, we study the motion of a
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
probe
in the background of the
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
solution.
Since Newman and Janis
Newman:1965tw-janis
,
it has been available that the field strength of the
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
solution takes the form

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}(x)\,=\,\frac{Q}{8\pi|\vec{x}{\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 1.00006pt}i\vec{a}|^{3}}\>{*}Y^{+}_{\mu\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{Q}{8\pi|\vec{x}{\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt}i\vec{a}|^{3}}\>{*}Y^{-}_{\mu\nu}(x)\,,

(355)

where
Q
is the charge parameter,

a^{\mu}
is a spacelike pseudovector encoding the ring radius,
and
u^{\mu}
is a timelike unit vector defining the stationary direction.
We have denoted

\displaystyle{*}Y^{\pm}_{\mu\nu}(x)\,=\,\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{u\wedge(x\mp ia)}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\hskip-0.50003pt{}_{\mu\nu}\,,

(356)

which
encodes the position three-vector
from imaginary centers
\pm ia
.
As disclosed by Ref.
nja
,
these centers are precisely
self-dual and anti-self-dual dyons.

The problem is still too hard.
With both self-dual and anti-self-dual fields,
the geometric series expansion of the Poisson bivector
does not truncate at a finite order,
so Eq. (
354
) describes time derivatives on its right-hand sides
unlike as in Eq. (
350
).

Hence we simplify the problem again by dropping
the anti-self-dual part of the field configuration in Eq. (
355
):

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}(x)\,=\,\frac{Q}{8\pi|\vec{x}{\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 1.00006pt}i\vec{a}|^{3}}\>{*}Y^{+}_{\mu\nu}(x){\quad\implies\quad}{*}F_{\mu\nu}(x)\,=\,+i\hskip 1.00006ptF_{\mu\nu}(x)\,.

(357)

This extracts the self-dual part of the
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
background in the precise sense.
It is not difficult to see that Eq. (
357
) describes

\displaystyle\vec{E}(\vec{x})\,=\,i\hskip 0.50003pt\vec{B}(\vec{x})\,=\,\frac{Q}{8\pi}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{\vec{x}{\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 1.00006pt}i\vec{a}}{|\vec{x}{\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 1.00006pt}i\vec{a}|^{3}}\,,

(358)

which is the electromagnetic field of a dyon
with electric charge
Q/2
and magnetic charge
-i\hskip 0.50003ptQ/2
:
a self-dual dyon.
The magnetic dipole moment of
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
arises precisely like
a
static
magnet made of monopoles:

Q\vec{a}=(-iQ/2)(2i\vec{a})
.
See Ref.
nja
for more details.

In short, we study
the motion of a
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
probe
in the background of a self-dual dyon.
The relevance to the original problem
is that the self-dual dyon
is a part of
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
.

In the self-dual dyon background,
we concern the combination

\displaystyle Y_{\mu\nu}(x)\,=\,Y^{+}_{\mu\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006ptY^{-}_{\mu\nu}(x)\,=\,\varepsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\hskip 1.00006ptx^{\rho}\hskip 0.50003ptu^{\sigma}-(u\wedge a)_{\mu\nu}\,.

(359)

It turns out that this defines a Killing-Yano tensor
yano1952some
such that

\displaystyle Y_{\mu\nu,\rho}(x)\,=\,\varepsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\hskip 1.00006ptu^{\sigma}\,=\,Y_{[\mu\nu,\rho]}(x)\,,\quad F^{\mu}{}_{\rho}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptY^{\rho}{}_{\nu}(x)\,=\,Y^{\mu}{}_{\rho}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptF^{\rho}{}_{\nu}(x)\,.

(360)

The first equation is the definition statement,
while the second equation describes a commuting property with the field strength.

In this background,
the
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
probe
obeys
the complexified EoM in Eq. (
351
):

\displaystyle\dot{z}^{\mu}\,=\,\hat{p}^{\mu}\,,\quad\dot{p}^{\mu}\,=\,\frac{q}{m}\,F^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(z)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\nu}\,,\quad\dot{\hat{y}}^{\mu}\,=\,\frac{q}{m}\,F^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(z)\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}^{\nu}\,,

(361)

Notably, the first two equations here
are identical to those of the charged scalar particle
up to the complexification
x\to z
,
which originated from the Poisson commutativity of holomorphic spinspacetime in Eq. (
348
).
Thus the well-known discourse on the scalar particle
Hughston:1972qf

is straightforwardly recycled as

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{Y^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(z)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\nu}}\,\Big)\,

\displaystyle=\,Y^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(z)\hskip 1.00006ptF^{\nu}{}_{\rho}(z)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\rho}+Y^{\mu}{}_{\nu,\rho}(z)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\nu}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{p}^{\rho}\,=\,F^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(z)\hskip 1.00006ptY^{\nu}{}_{\rho}(z)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\rho}\,,

(362)

showing that the precessions of

p
and
Y(z)\hskip 1.00006ptp
are synchronized.

Crucially,
the precession of the spin length pseudovector
\hat{y}
is also synchronized with
p
.
Now we find three vectors singing in unison,

p
,
Y(z)\hskip 1.00006ptp
, and
\hat{y}
.
It is then immediate that

\displaystyle R\,=\,p_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptY^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(z)\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{y}^{\nu}\,,\quad C\,=\,-p_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptY^{\mu}{}_{\rho}(z)\hskip 1.00006ptY^{\rho}{}_{\nu}(z)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{\nu}

(363)

are conserved quantities:
the all-orders-in-spin completions of
the Rüdiger
Rudiger:1981uu
;
Rudiger:1984er
and Carter
Carter:1968ks
constants.

Moreover, the self-dual dyon background exhibits isometries
\mathbb{R}\times\mathrm{SO}(3)
.
Again, we immediately deduce the
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
probe’s conserved charge
by direct dynamical Newman-Janis shift of the Coulomb probe’s conserved charge in Eq. (
305
):

\displaystyle Q\,=\,p_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptK^{\mu}(z)+{\alpha}(z)\,.

(364)

The symmetry algebra between these Newman-Janis shifted Killing charges
are the same as in the non-spinning case,
crucially because of the Poisson commutativity

\{\hskip 0.50003pt{z^{\mu}},{z^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}=0

that we have had to emphasize.

Using
Eqs. (
363
) and (
364
),
one can show that
the dynamics is exactly solvable (integrable).
See Refs.
probe-nj
;
njmagic.11
to grasp more details for the superintegrability.

In summary, we have learned that
the self-dual sector of the
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
-
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
binary problem is exactly solvable,
based on
direct dynamical Newman-Janis shifts of the non-spinning probe’s conserved charges
as well as
the synchronization of orbital and spin precessions.

This is yet an idealized discussion.
How should one approach the original problem of the
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
-
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
binary system, then?

A reasonable pathway may be perturbing away from the self-dual sector
where exact all-orders expressions for the conserved charges are known:
the googly agenda
probe-nj
.

10
Summary

1.

The study of relativistic massive spinning particle models boasts a long history
and has seen renewed attention
in the context of point-particle effective theory of compact astrophysical objects.
An extensive zoo of models has emerged,
featuring different implementations of spin
with varying degrees of redundancy.

[2]

Poincaré symmetry implies
the existence of universal variables
(x,\hat{y},p)

in any Hamiltonian formulation of a free massive spinning particle,
satisfying the universal Poisson bracket relation.
The space of these variables admits
ten- or eight-dimensional symplectic submanifolds
tracing back to Souriau’s elementary realization of spin.

[3]

The existence of the universal variables
(x,\hat{y},p)

and their Poisson algebra
is concretely substantiated in four example models:
vector oscillator, spinor oscillator, spherical top, and massive twistor.
The vector oscillator describes the spin phase space
T^{*}S^{2}

and admits the spinor oscillator as a reformulation with no spin redundancy.
The spherical top describes the spin phase space
T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)

and admits the massive twistor as a reformulation with less spin redundancy.

[4]

The hierarchy

T^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)\supset T^{*}S^{2}\supset S^{2}

of spin phase spaces
arises by
explicit identifications of the constraints
in the symplectic reduction and Dirac bracket frameworks.

[5]

The BMT equations are direct consequences of the universal Poisson bracket relation.
Universal recipes for the minimal and dipolar couplings
are identified
in the symplectic perturbation framework.

(6)

The equivalence principle asserts
the existence of
universal variables
(x,\hat{y},p)

in any Hamiltonian formulation of a massive spinning particle in general relativity,
which satisfy the universal covariant Poisson bracket relation.
The MPD and QMPD equations are direct consequences of this covariant Poisson bracket relation.
Universal recipes for the minimal and quadrupolar couplings
are identified
in the covariant symplectic perturbation framework.

(7)

To ensure that
physical observables are ignorant of one’s choice of SSC,
the spherical top model must be coupled to external fields
in the spin gauge-invariant manner.
The spin gauge-invariant variables,
such as the general-relativistic physical center coordinates,
describe highly nonlinear formulae
when expressed in terms of the defining variables.
Noncovariant SSCs necessitate adding infinite towers of fictitious interaction terms in the Lagrangian.
The spin gauge algebra and flow
are preserved by the interactions.

(8)

The existence of the general-relativistic universal variables
(x,\hat{y},p)

and their Poisson algebra
is explicitly proven in the four example models.

(9)

Model-independent conserved charges
are direct consequences of the universal Poisson and covariant Poisson bracket relations.

In the context of PM expansion,
equivalent models produce classical eikonals (Magnusians)
that generate the same effect on the
(x,\hat{y},p)
space.
Models with less redundancy and linear phase space
can be preferred in general.
Spinorial models might be optimal for studying all-orders-in-spin dynamics of black holes.
When reading off the Feynman rules in noncovariant SSCs,
it is important to include the extra vertices due to the fictitious terms.
Such fictitious vertices cannot be neglected
in the PN effective theory computation of the NNLO spin-squared potential,
for example.

From the universal variables,
one builds
interaction Lagrangians encoding
not only linear couplings to all multipole orders
but also nonlinear couplings.
Nonlinear couplings are incorporated
in the forms of explicit contact terms or spin-derivative terms.

The universal Poisson bracket relation admits a radically simple reformulation in terms of the complex spinspacetime coordinates
z=x\hskip 0.50003pt{\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt}i\hat{y}

which exhibits Poisson-commutativity
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{z},{z}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}=0
.
The simplicity of black hole couplings
and
the Newman-Janis shift
are attributed to this universal complex geometry
instead of model-specific arguments.
The universal derivation of
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
EoM is given.
An exactly solvable sector of the
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
-
\kern-0.74997pt\sqrt{\text{Kerr\hskip 0.50003pt}}
binary problem is identified
in an idealized setup
via direct dynamical Newman-Janis shifts of conserved charges.
